| SIR CLAUDE | Of course, she knows you were wanting to retire,<br><br><br>As we had some discussion about replacing you.<br>But you know she regards you — [[motifs/well\|well]], completely<br>As one of the household. |
| EGGERSON | That’s a great compliment. |
| SIR CLAUDE | And [[motifs/well\|well]] deserved; but rather inconvenient<br><br><br>When it comes to appointing a successor.<br>Makes it very difficult to replace you.<br>She thinks she ought to have a hand in the choosing;<br>And besides, she is convinced that she, of all people,<br>Is a better judge of character than I am. |
| EGGERSON | Oh, I wouldn’t say that, Sir Claude!<br><br><br>She has too much respect for your business genius.<br>But it’s true she believes she has what she calls ‘guidance’. |
| SIR CLAUDE | Guidance. That’s worse than believing in her judgment:<br><br><br>We could argue about that. You can’t argue with guidance.<br>But if she appears to be puzzled, or annoyed<br>At my making the appointment during her absence,<br>You must say you had to leave under medical orders.<br>She’s always been concerned about your state of health,<br>So she’ll be sympathetic. And as for Colby —<br>Say that Mr. Simpkins was highly recommended,<br>And say that I had to make a quick decision<br>Because he’d had another very tempting offer.<br>Something like that. Don’t make too much of it.<br>And I rather [[concepts/hope\|hope]] that she will take to him at once:<br>If so, she is certain to come to believe<br>That she chose him herself. By the way, don’t forget<br>To let her know that he’s very musical.<br>She can take him to concerts. But don’t overdo it! |
| EGGERSON | I’ll remember that. Music. |
| SIR CLAUDE | And by the way,<br>How much have you actually told him about her?<br>You remember,1 asked you to prepare him a little;<br>There are some things you could say better than I could,<br>And ways in which you could reassure him<br>Better than I. He’s more at ease with you<br>Than he is with me. |
| EGGERSON | Oh, you mustn’t say that!<br><br><br>Though I’ve done my best to gain his confidence.<br>I did mention her interest in Light from the East. |
| SIR CLAUDE | And the Book of Revelation? And the Wisdom of Atlantis? |
| EGGERSON | [[motifs/well\|Well]], to tell the [[concepts/truth\|truth]], Sir Claude, I only touched on these matters,<br><br><br>They’re much too deep for me. And I thought, Mr. Simpkins,<br>He’s highly educated. He’ll soon begin to grasp them.<br>No, I haven’t told him much about Lady Elizabeth.<br>But there’s one thing I should like to know —<br>If you don’t mind — before I go to meet her.<br>How soon do you propose to … explain Mr. Simpkins?<br><br><br>Regularize his position in the household?<br>You told me that was your eventual intention. |
| SIR CLAUDE | When — or indeed whether — I reveal his [[concepts/identity\|identity]]<br><br><br>Depends on how she takes to him. This afternoon<br>She will only learn that you have finally retired<br>And that you have a young successor,<br>A Mr. [[characters/colby-simpkins\|Colby Simpkins]]. |
| EGGERSON | Merely Mr. Simpkins. |
| SIR CLAUDE | The reasons for starting him during her absence<br><br><br>Are perfectly clear. But beyond that point<br>I haven’t yet explained my plans to you.<br>Why I’ve never told her about him,<br>The reason for meeting him as merely Mr. Simpkins,<br>Is, that she has a strong maternal instinct … |
| EGGERSON | I realise that. |
| SIR CLAUDE | Which has always been thwarted. |
| EGGERSON | I’m sure it’s been a grief to both of you<br><br><br>That you’ve never had children. |
| SIR CLAUDE | No worse, Eggerson,<br><br><br>Than for you and your wife, to have had a son<br>Lost in action, and his [[motifs/grave\|grave]] unknown. |
| EGGERSON | And you’re thinking no [[concepts/doubt\|doubt]] that Lady Elizabeth<br><br><br>Would be put in mind of the [[archetypes/child\|child]] she lost. |
| SIR CLAUDE | In a very different way, yes. You might say mislaid,<br>Since the father is dead, and there’s no way of tracing it.<br>Yes, I was thinking of her missing [[archetypes/child\|child]]:<br>In the circumstances, that might make her jealous.<br>I’ve explained all this to Colby — Mr. Simpkins. |
| EGGERSON | I see what you mean. |
| SIR CLAUDE | She must get to like him first:<br><br><br>And then, Eggerson, I am not unhopeful<br>That, under the impression that he is an [[archetypes/orphan\|orphan]],<br>She will want us to adopt him. |
| EGGERSON | Adopt him! Yes, indeed,<br><br><br>That would be the solution. Yes, quite ideal. |
| SIR CLAUDE | I’m glad you agree. Your support will be helpful. |
| EGGERSON | I’m sure I shall be very happy to commend him. |
| SIR CLAUDE | You mustn’t overdo it! But your approval matters.<br><br><br>You know she thinks the world of your opinion. |
| EGGERSON | [[motifs/well\|Well]], I believe that once or twice, perhaps …<br><br><br>But I’m afraid you overrate my influence.<br>I have never been able to make her like Miss Angel;<br>She becomes abstracted, whenever I mention her. |
| SIR CLAUDE | But she knew about Lucasta — Miss Angel, from the start.<br><br><br>That was one difficulty. And there are others.<br>For one, they’re both of them women. |
| EGGERSON | True. |
| SIR CLAUDE | But I don’t think she takes much notice of Miss Angel.<br><br><br>She just doesn’t see her. And Miss Angel<br>Will soon be getting married, I expect. |
| EGGERSON | And so I [[concepts/hope\|hope]]. A most suitable arrangement.<br><br><br>But will you tell me this: if it comes to the point<br>At which Lady Elizabeth wants to adopt him —<br>An admirable solution — then what follows?<br>Will you let her know, then, that Mr. Simpkins<br>Is actually your son? |
| SIR CLAUDE | That’s where I’m in the dark.<br><br><br>I simply can’t guess what her reaction would be.<br>There’s a lot I don’t understand about my wife.<br>There’s always something one’s ignorant of<br>About anyone, however [[motifs/well\|well]] one knows them;<br>And that may be something of the greatest importance.<br>It’s when you’re sure you understand a person<br>That you’re liable to make the worst mistake about him.<br>As a matter of fact, there’s a lot I don’t know<br>About you, Eggerson, although we worked together<br>For nearly thirty years. |
| EGGERSON | Nearly thirty-one.<br><br><br>But now you put it so convincingly,<br>I must admit there’s a lot that I don’t understand<br><br><br>About my wife. |
| SIR CLAUDE | And just as much<br><br><br>She doesn’t know about you. And just as much<br>You don’t know about me — I’m not so sure of that!<br>My rule is to remember that I understand nobody,<br>But on the other hand never to be sure<br>That they don’t understand me — a good deal better<br>Than I should care to think, perhaps. |
| EGGERSON | And do I infer<br><br><br>That you’re not sure you understand Mr. Simpkins, either? |
| SIR CLAUDE | A timely reminder. You may have to repeat it.<br><br><br>But he should be back by now. And then I’ll leave you.<br>I must telephone to Amsterdam, and possibly to [[settings/paris\|Paris]].<br>But when you return with Lady Elizabeth<br>I’ll be ready waiting to introduce him. |
| *(didascalia)* | [Enter [[characters/colby-simpkins\|COLBY SIMPKINS]] with briefcase] |
| SIR CLAUDE | Ah, Colby, I was just saying to Eggerson<br><br><br>It was [[motifs/time\|time]] you were back. Was your morning satisfactory? |
| COLBY | I’ve got what you wanted, Sir Claude. Good afternoon,<br><br><br>Mr. Eggerson. I was afraid I’d miss you. |
| EGGERSON | I’m off in half an hour, Mr. Simpkins. |
| SIR CLAUDE | I’ll leave you now. But when Eggerson comes back<br><br><br>With Lady Elizabeth, I will rejoin you. |
| *(didascalia)* | [Exit SIR CLAUDE] |
| COLBY | I’m glad you don’t have to leave just yet.<br><br><br>I’m rather nervous about this meeting.<br>You’ve told me very little about Lady Elizabeth,<br>And Sir Claude himself hasn’t told me very much:<br>So I’ve no idea how I ought to behave.<br>B. Kaghan has told me something about her,<br>But that’s rather alarming. |
| EGGERSON | Mr. Kaghan is prejudiced.<br><br><br>He’s never hit it off with Lady Elizabeth.<br>Don’t listen to him. He understands Sir Claude,<br>And he’s always been very grateful to Sir Claude,<br>As he ought to be. Sir Claude picked him out<br>And gave him his start. And he’s made the most of it —<br>That I will say. An encouraging example<br>For you, Mr. Simpkins. He’ll be a power in the [[motifs/city\|City]]!<br>And he has a heart of gold. But not to beat about the bush,<br>He’s rather a rough diamond. Very free and easy ways;<br>And Lady Elizabeth has never taken to him.<br>But you, Mr. Simpkins, that’s very different. |
| COLBY | I don’t know why it should be so different.<br><br><br>I like B. Kaghan. I’ve found him very helpful<br>And very good company apart from business. |
| EGGERSON | Oh yes, Mr. Kaghan is very good company.<br><br><br>He makes me laugh sometimes. I don’t laugh easily.<br>Quite a humorist, he is. In fact, Mrs. E.<br>Sometimes says to me: ‘Eggerson, why can’t you make me laugh<br>The way B. Kaghan did?’ She’s only met him once;<br>But do you know, he began addressing her as Muriel —<br>Within the first ten minutes! I was horrified.<br>But she actually liked it. Muriel is her name.<br><br><br>He has a way with the ladies, you know.<br>But with Lady Elizabeth he wasn’t so successful.<br>She once referred to him as ‘undistinguished’;<br>But with you, as I said, it will be very different.<br>She’ll see at once that you’re a man of culture;<br>And besides, she’s very musical. |
| COLBY | Thank you for the warning! |
| EGGERSON | So if you don’t mind, I shall mention at once<br><br><br>That you are a musician. |
| COLBY | I’ll be on my guard. |
| EGGERSON | Your music will certainly be a great asset<br><br><br>With Lady Elizabeth. I envy you that.<br>I’ve always sung in our voluntary choir<br>And at the [[carol]] service. But I wish I was musical. |
| COLBY | I still don’t feel very [[motifs/well\|well]] prepared for meeting her. |
| *(didascalia)* | [A loud knock. Enter B. KAGHAN] |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter B. Kaghan. Hello Colby! |
| KAGHAN | And hello Eggers! I’m glad to find you here.<br>It’s lucky for Colby. |
| EGGERSON | How so Mr. Kaghan? |
| KAGHAN | Because Lucasta’s with me! The usual catastrophe.<br><br><br>She’s come to pry some cash from the money-box.<br>Bankrupt again! So I thought I’d better bring her<br>And come upstairs ahead, to ease the shock for Colby.<br>But as you’re here, Eggers, I can just relax.<br>I’m going to enjoy the game from the sidelines. |
| *(didascalia)* | [Enter LUCASTA ANGEL] |
| LUCASTA | Eggy, I’ve lost my job! |
| EGGERSON | Again, Miss Angel? |
| LUCASTA | Yes, again! And serve them right! |
| EGGERSON | You have been, I presume, persistently unpunctual. |
| LUCASTA | You’re wrong, Eggy. It’s rank injustice.<br><br><br>Two months I’d gone on filing those papers<br>Which no one ever wanted — at least, not till yesterday.<br>Then, just by bad luck, the boss did want a letter<br>And I couldn’t find it. And then he got suspicious<br>And asked for things I’m sure he didn’t want —<br>Just to make trouble! And I couldn’t find one of them.<br>But they’re all filed somewhere, I’m sure, so why bother?<br>But who’s this, Eggy? Is it [[characters/colby-simpkins\|Colby Simpkins]]?<br>Introduce him, one or the other of you. |
| EGGERSON | Mr. Simpkins, Miss Angel. As you know, Miss Angel,<br><br><br>Mr. Simpkins has taken over my duties. |
| LUCASTA | And does he know that I’m one of his duties?<br><br><br>Have you prepared him for taking me over?<br><br><br>Did you know that, Colby? I’m Lucasta.<br>It’s only Eggy calls me Miss Angel,<br>Just to annoy me. Don’t you agree<br>That Lucasta suits me better? |
| COLBY | I’m sure they both suit you. |
| LUCASTA | Snubbed again! I suppose I asked for it.<br><br><br>That’s what comes of being cursed with a name like Angel.<br>I’m thinking of changing it. But, Colby,<br>Do you know that I’m one of your responsibilities? |
| COLBY | No, I’m afraid I didn’t know that. |
| EGGERSON | You mustn’t give way to her, Mr. Simpkins.<br>I never do. I always say<br>That if you give Miss Angel an inch<br>She’ll take an ell. |
| LUCASTA | L. for Lucasta.<br><br><br>Go on, Eggy. Don’t mind him, Colby.<br>Colby, are you married? |
| COLBY | No, I’m not married. |
| LUCASTA | Then I don’t mind being seen with you in public.<br><br><br>You may take me out to dinner. A working girl like me<br>Is often very hungry — living on a pittance —<br>Cooking a sausage on a gas ring … |
| EGGERSON | You mustn’t believe a word she says. |
| LUCASTA | Mr. Simpkins is going to believe all I say,<br><br><br>Mr. Eggerson. And I know he’ll be nice to me<br><br><br>When you’re out of the way. Why don’t you let him speak?<br>Eggy’s really quite human, Colby.<br>It’s only that he’s terrified of Mrs. Eggerson;<br>That’s why he’s never asked me out to lunch. |
| EGGERSON | We will leave Mrs. Eggerson out of this, Miss Angel. |
| LUCASTA | That’s what he always says, Colby,<br><br><br>When I mention Mrs. Eggerson. He never fails to rise.<br>B.! What have you told Colby about me? |
| KAGHAN | It’s no use telling anybody about you:<br><br><br>Nobody’d ever believe in your existence<br>Until they met you. Colby’s still reeling.<br>It’s going to be my responsibility,<br>As your fiancé, to protect Colby from you.<br>But first, let’s cope with the financial crisis. |
| LUCASTA | Yes, Eggy, will you break the sad news to Claude?<br><br><br>Meanwhile, you’ll have to raid the till for me. I’m starving. |
| KAGHAN | I’ve just given her lunch. The problem with Lucasta<br><br><br>Is how to keep her fed between meals. |
| LUCASTA | B., you’re a beast. I’ve a very small appetite.<br><br><br>But the point is, that I’m penniless. |
| KAGHAN | She’s had a week’s salary in lieu of notice. |
| LUCASTA | B., remember you’re only my fiancé on approval.<br><br><br>Can I have some money, Eggy? |
| EGGERSON | I’m no longer in charge<br><br><br>And that duty has not devolved on Mr. Simpkins:<br><br><br>Sir Claude intends to deal with these matters himself.<br>You will have to ask Sir Claude. But I’ll speak to him<br>When I return from Northolt. |
| LUCASTA | You’re going to meet Lizzie? |
| EGGERSON | I am meeting Lady Elizabeth at Northolt. |
| LUCASTA | [[motifs/well\|Well]], I don’t propose to be on the scene when she comes. |
| KAGHAN | And I don’t propose to leave you with Colby.<br><br><br>He’s had enough for one day. Take my advice, Colby.<br>Never allow Lucasta the slightest advantage<br>Or she’ll exploit it. You have to be tough with her;<br>She’s hard as nails. Now I’ll take her off your hands.<br>I’ll show you how it’s done. Come along, Lucasta,<br>I’m going to make a day of it, and take you out to tea. |
| LUCASTA | I’m dying for my tea. The strain of this crisis<br><br><br>Has been too much for me. Another [[motifs/time\|time]], Colby.<br>I’ll ring you up, and let you take me out to lunch. |
| *(didascalia)* | [Exit LUCASTA] |
| KAGHAN | Take it easy, Colby. You’ll get used to her. |
| *(didascalia)* | [Exit KAGHAN] |
| COLBY | Egg … Mr. Eggerson! |
| EGGERSON | Yes, Mr. Simpkins? |
| COLBY | You seem to me sane. And I think I am. |
| EGGERSON | I have no [[concepts/doubt\|doubt]] on either point, none at all. |
| COLBY | And B. Kaghan has always seemed to me sane. |
| EGGERSON | I should call him the very picture of [[Sanity\|sanity]]. |
| COLBY | But you never warned me about Miss Angel.<br><br><br>What about her? |
| EGGERSON | Oh, Miss Angel.<br><br><br>She’s rather flighty. But she has a good heart. |
| COLBY | But does she address [[characters/sir-claude-mulhammer\|Sir Claude Mulhammer]]<br><br><br>As Claude? To his face? |
| EGGERSON | She does indeed. |
| COLBY | And does she call Lady Elizabeth Lizzie? |
| EGGERSON | [[motifs/well\|Well]], not in her presence. Not when I’ve been there.<br><br><br>No, I don’t think she would. But she does call her Lizzie,<br>Sometimes, to Sir Claude. And do you know —<br>I think it amuses him. |
| COLBY | [[motifs/well\|Well]], perhaps I’ll be amused.<br><br><br>But it did make my head spin — all those first names<br>The first [[motifs/time\|time]] I met her. I’m not used to it. |
| EGGERSON | You’ll soon get used to it. You’ll be calling me Eggers<br><br><br>Before you know it! |
| COLBY | I shouldn’t [[concepts/wonder\|wonder]].<br><br><br>I nearly did, a moment ago.<br>Then I’d have been certain I’d lost my reason:<br>Her influence is perfectly frightening.<br>But tell me about Lu … Miss Angel:<br>What’s her connection with this household? |
| EGGERSON | [[motifs/well\|Well]]. A kind of fiduciary relationship.<br><br><br>No, I don’t think that’s quite the right term.<br>She’s no money of her own, as you may have gathered;<br>But I think her father was a friend of Sir Claude’s,<br>And he’s made himself responsible for her.<br>In any case, he’s behaved like a father —<br>A very generous man, is Sir Claude.<br>To tell the [[concepts/truth\|truth]], she’s something of a thorn in his flesh,<br>Always losing her jobs, because she won’t stick to them.<br>He gives her an allowance — very adequate indeed,<br>Though she’s always in debt. But you needn’t worry<br>About her, Mr. Simpkins. She’ll marry Mr. Kaghan<br>In the end. He’s a man who gets his own way,<br>And I think he can manage her. If anyone can. |
| COLBY | But is she likely to be a nuisance? |
| EGGERSON | Not unless you give her encouragement.<br><br><br>I have never encouraged her. |
| COLBY | But you have Mrs. Eggerson. |
| EGGERSON | Yes, she’s a great protection. And I have my [[motifs/garden\|garden]]<br><br><br>To protect me against Mrs. E. That’s my joke. |
| COLBY | [[motifs/well\|Well]], I’ve never met anyone like Miss Angel. |
| EGGERSON | You’ll get used to her, Mr. Simpkins.<br><br><br>[[motifs/time\|Time]] works wonders, that’s what I always say.<br>But I don’t expect you’ll have to see much of her:<br>That responsibility’s not on your shoulders.<br>Lady Elizabeth, now, that’s different. |
| COLBY | At least, I don’t suppose Lady Elizabeth<br><br><br>Can be quite so unusual as Miss Angel. |
| EGGERSON | O yes, Mr. Simpkins, much more unusual. |
| COLBY | Oh! |
| EGGERSON | [[motifs/well\|Well]], as I told you, she really is a lady,<br><br><br>Rather a grande dame, as the French say.<br>That’s what Sir Claude admires about her.<br>He said to me once, in a moment of confidence —<br>He’d just come back from a public luncheon —<br>‘Eggerson’, he said, ‘I wanted a lady,<br>And I’m perfectly satisfied with the bargain.’<br>Of course it’s true that her family connections<br>Have sometimes been useful. But he didn’t think of that:<br>He’s not petty-minded — though nothing escapes him.<br>And such a generous heart! He’s rather a Socialist.<br>I’m a staunch Conservative, myself. |
| COLBY | But is Lady Elizabeth very unusual<br><br><br>In any other way, besides being a lady? |
| EGGERSON | Why, yes, indeed, I must admit she is.<br><br><br>Most of her oddities are perfectly harmless.<br>You’ll soon get used to them. That’s what Sir Claude said:<br>‘[[works/humour-(chesterton)\|Humour]] her, Eggerson,’ he said, ‘[[Humour\|humour]] her.’<br>But she has one trait that I think I did touch on:<br>She’s very absent-minded. |
| COLBY | I [[concepts/hope\|hope]] you don’t mean,<br><br><br>She has lapses of [[motifs/memory\|memory]]? |
| EGGERSON | I didn’t mean that.<br><br><br>No. She hasn’t very much [[motifs/memory\|memory]] to lose,<br>Though she sometimes remembers when you least expect it.<br>But she does forget things. And she likes to travel,<br>Mostly for her health. And when she’s abroad<br>She is apt to buy a [[house]]. And then goes away<br>And forgets all about it. That can be complicated<br>And very costly. I’ve had some rare adventures!<br>I remember long ago, saying to Mrs. E.,<br>When we’d bought our [[house]] in Joshua Park<br>(On a mortgage, of course) ‘now we’ve settled down<br>All the travel I want is up to the [[motifs/city\|City]]<br><br><br>And back to Joshua Park in the evening,<br>And once a year our holiday at Dawlish’.<br>And to think that was only the beginning of my travels!<br>It’s been a very unusual privilege<br>To see as much of Europe as I have,<br>Getting Lady Elizabeth out of her difficulties. |
| COLBY | Perhaps she won’t even arrive by this plane. |
| EGGERSON | Oh, that could happen. She sometimes gets lost,<br>Or loses her ticket, or even her passport.<br>But let’s not be crossing any bridges<br>Until we come to them. That’s what I always say.<br><br><br>And I’m sure you’ll like her. She’s such a lady!<br><br><br>And what’s more, she has a good heart. |
| COLBY | Everybody seems to be kind-hearted.<br><br><br>But there’s one thing I do believe, Mr. Eggerson:<br>That you have a kind heart. And I’m convinced<br><br><br>That you always contrive to think the best of everyone. |
| EGGERSON | You’ll come to find that I’m right, I assure you. |
| *(didascalia)* | [Enter SIR CLAUDE] |
| SIR CLAUDE | Hello! Still here? It’s [[motifs/time\|time]] you were off. |
| EGGERSON | I’m just going. There’s plenty of [[motifs/time\|time]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | [Looks at his watch] |
| EGGERSON | I’ll arrive at the airport with minutes to spare,<br>And besides, there’s the Customs. That’ll take her a [[motifs/time\|time]],<br>From my experience.<br>LADY ELIZABETH MULHAMMER’S voice off.* Just open that case, I want something out of it.<br><br><br>Unwrap that — It’s a bottle of medicine.<br>Now, Parkman, will you give it to the driver?<br>He tells me that he suffers from chronic catarrh. |
| SIR CLAUDE | Hello! What’s that? |
| *(didascalia)* | [Opens door on to landing and listens] |
| SIR CLAUDE | She’s here, Eggerson! That’s her voice.<br>Where is she? Oh, she’s gone out again. |
| *(didascalia)* | [Goes to the [[motifs/window\|window]] and looks down on the street] |
| SIR CLAUDE | She’s having a conversation with the [[cabman]].<br>What can they be talking about? She’s coming in!<br>LADY ELIZABETH MULHAMMER’S voice off.* No, [[characters/gertrude\|Gertrude]], I haven’t had any lunch,<br><br><br>And I don’t want it now. Just bring me some tea.<br>Nothing with it. No, I forgot:<br>You haven’t learned yet how to make tea properly.<br>A cup of black coffee. Is Sir Claude at [[motifs/home\|home]]?<br>I’ll speak to him first. |
| SIR CLAUDE | Good heavens, Eggerson, what can have happened? |
| EGGERSON | It’s perfectly amazing. Let me go down to meet her. |
| SIR CLAUDE | Where ought we to be? What ought we to be doing?<br><br><br>EGGERSON [at the open door]. She’s speaking to the parlourmaid. She’s coming up. |
| SIR CLAUDE | Colby, sit at the desk, and pick up some papers.<br><br><br>We must look as if we’d been engaged in business. |
| *(didascalia)* | [Enter LADY ELIZABETH MULHAMMER] |
| SIR CLAUDE | EGGERSON & SIR CLAUDE [simultaneously]. Lady Elizabeth!<br><br><br>Elizabeth! |
| SIR CLAUDE | What on earth has happened? |
| EGGERSON | Lady Elizabeth! This is most surprising. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | What’s surprising, Eggerson? I’ve arrived, that’s all. |
| EGGERSON | I was just starting for Northolt to meet you. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | That was very thoughtful of you, Eggerson,<br><br><br>But quite unnecessary. And besides,<br>I didn’t come by air. I arrived at Victoria. |
| SIR CLAUDE | Do you mean to say that you changed your ticket? |
| EGGERSON | Yes, how did you manage to change your ticket? |
| LADY ELIZABETH | I went to the agency and got them to change it.<br><br><br>I can’t understand why you’re both so surprised.<br>You know I’m a very experienced traveller. |
| SIR CLAUDE | Oh yes, of course we know that, Elizabeth.<br><br><br>But why did you change your plans? |
| LADY ELIZABETH | Because of Mildred Deverell.<br><br><br>She’s been having the treatment with me,<br>And she can’t go by air — she says it makes her sea-sick;<br>So we took the [[motifs/night\|night]] train, and did the Channel crossing.<br>But who is this young man? His face is familiar. |
| SIR CLAUDE | This young man is Eggerson’s successor.<br><br><br>You know that Eggerson’s been meaning to retire … |
| EGGERSON | Under medical orders, Lady Elizabeth:<br><br><br>The doctor made it very imperative … |
| SIR CLAUDE | Mr. Simpkins had very strong recommendations … |
| EGGERSON | And at the same [[motifs/time\|time]], he had another tempting offer:<br><br><br>So we had to make a quick decision. |
| SIR CLAUDE | I didn’t want to bother you, during your treatment … |
| EGGERSON | And Mr. Simpkins is much more highly qualified<br><br><br>Than I am, to be a confidential clerk.<br>Besides, he’s very musical. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | Musical?<br><br><br>Isn’t this the young man I interviewed<br>And recommended to Sir Claude? Of course it is.<br>I remember saying: ‘He has a good aura.’<br>I remember people’s auras almost better than their faces.<br>What did you say his name was? |
| SIR CLAUDE | [[characters/colby-simpkins\|Colby Simpkins]].<br><br><br>LADY ELIZABETH [counting on her fingers]. Thirteen letters. That’s very auspicious —<br><br><br>Contrary to what most people think.<br>You should be artistic. But you look rather frail.<br>I must give you lessons in the art of health.<br>Where is your [[motifs/home\|home]], Mr. Colby? |
| COLBY | Simpkins. |
| EGGERSON | Mr. [[characters/colby-simpkins\|Colby Simpkins]]. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | I prefer Colby.<br><br><br>Where are you living? |
| SIR CLAUDE | His [[motifs/home\|home]]’s outside [[settings/london\|London]].<br><br><br>But I want to have him closer at hand —<br>You know what a bother it’s been for Eggerson —<br>So I’m having the flat in the mews done over. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | But all in the wrong colours, I’m sure. My husband<br><br><br>Does not understand the importance of colour<br>For our spiritual life, Mr. Colby.<br>Neither, I regret to say, does Eggerson.<br>What colour have you chosen, between you? |
| SIR CLAUDE | I thought a primrose yellow would be cheerful. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | Just what I expected. A primrose yellow<br><br><br>Would be absolutely baneful to Mr. Colby.<br>He needs a light mauve. I shall see about that.<br>But not today. I shall go and rest now.<br>In a sleeping-car it is quite impossible<br>To get one’s quiet hour. A quiet hour a day<br>Is most essential, Dr. Rebmann says. |
| SIR CLAUDE | Rebmann? I thought it was a Dr. Leroux. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | Dr. Leroux is in Lausanne.<br><br><br>I have been in Zürich, under Dr. Rebmann. |
| SIR CLAUDE | But you were going out to Dr. Leroux<br><br><br>In Lausanne. What made you go to Zürich? |
| LADY ELIZABETH | Why, I’d no sooner got to Lausanne<br><br><br>Than whom should I meet but Mildred Deverell.<br>She was going on to Zürich. So she said: ‘Come to Zürich!<br>There’s a wonderful doctor who teaches mind control.’<br>So on I went to Zürich. |
| SIR CLAUDE | So on you went to Zürich.<br><br><br>But I thought that the doctor in Lausanne taught mind control? |
| LADY ELIZABETH | No, Claude, he only teaches thought control.<br><br><br>Mind control is a different matter:<br>It’s more advanced. But I wrote you all about it. |
| SIR CLAUDE | It’s true, you did send me postcards from Zürich;<br><br><br>But you know that I can’t decipher your writing.<br>I like to have the cards, just to know where you are<br>By reading the postmark. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | But Claude, I’m glad to find<br><br><br>That you’ve taken my advice. |
| SIR CLAUDE | Your advice? About what? |
| LADY ELIZABETH | To engage Mr. Colby. I really am distressed!<br><br><br>This is not the first sign that I’ve noticed<br>Of your [[motifs/memory\|memory]] failing. I must persuade you<br>To have a course of treatment with Dr. Rebmann —<br>No, at your stage, I think, with Dr. Leroux.<br>Don’t you remember, I said before I left:<br>‘Trust my guidance for once, and engage that young man?’<br>[[motifs/well\|Well]], that was Mr. Colby. |
| SIR CLAUDE | Oh, I see.<br><br><br>Yes, now I am beginning to remember.<br>I must have acted on your guidance. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | I must explain to you, Mr. Colby,<br><br><br>That I am to share you with my husband.<br>You shall have tea with me tomorrow,<br>And then I shall tell you about my committees.<br>I must go and rest now. |
| SIR CLAUDE | Yes, you go and rest.<br><br><br>I’m in the middle of some business with Mr…. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | Colby! |
| *(didascalia)* | [Exit LADY ELIZABETH] |
| SIR CLAUDE | She actually went and changed her own ticket.<br><br><br>It’s something unheard of. |
| EGGERSON | Amazing, isn’t it! |
| SIR CLAUDE | If this is what the doctor in Zürich has done for her,<br><br><br>I give him full marks. [[motifs/well\|Well]], Eggerson,<br>I seem to have brought you up to [[settings/london\|London]] for nothing. |
| EGGERSON | Oh, not for nothing! I wouldn’t have missed it.<br><br><br>And besides, as I told you, I’ve done some shopping.<br>But I’d better be off now. Mr. Simpkins —<br>If anything should turn up unexpected<br><br><br>And you find yourself non-plussed, you must get me on the phone.<br>If I’m not in the [[house]], I’ll be out in the [[motifs/garden\|garden]].<br>And I’ll slip up to town any day, if you want me.<br>In fact, Mrs. E. said: ‘I wish he’d ring us up!<br>I’m sure he has a very cultivated voice.’ |
| COLBY | Thank you very much, I will. It’s reassuring<br><br><br>To know that I have you always at my back<br>If I get into trouble. But I [[concepts/hope\|hope]]<br>That I shan’t have to call upon you often. |
| EGGERSON | Oh, and I forgot … Mrs. E. keeps saying:<br><br><br>‘Why don’t you ask him out to dinner one [[characters/sunday\|Sunday]]?’<br>But I say: ‘We couldn’t ask him to come<br>All the way to Joshua Park, at this [[motifs/time\|time]] of year!’<br>I said: ‘Let’s think about it in the [[motifs/spring\|Spring]]<br>When the [[motifs/garden\|garden]] will really be a treat to look at.’<br>[[motifs/well\|Well]], I’ll be going. |
| SIR CLAUDE | Goodbye, and thank you, Eggerson. |
| EGGERSON | Good day, Sir Claude. Good day, Mr. Simpkins. |
| *(didascalia)* | [Exit EGGERSON] |
| SIR CLAUDE | [[motifs/well\|Well]], Colby! I’ve been calling you Mr. Simpkins<br><br><br>In public, till now, as a matter of prudence.<br>As we arranged. But after two months —<br>And as my wife insists upon your being Mr. Colby —<br>I shall begin to call you Colby with everyone. |
| COLBY | I’m sure that will make it easier for both of us. |
| SIR CLAUDE | Her sudden arrival was very disconcerting:<br><br><br>As you gather, such a thing never happened before.<br>So the meeting didn’t go quite the way I’d intended;<br>And yet I believe that it’s all for the best.<br>It went off very [[motifs/well\|well]]. It’s very obvious<br>That she took to you at once. |
| COLBY | Did she really think<br><br><br>That she had seen me before? |
| SIR CLAUDE | Impossible to tell.<br><br><br>The point is that she’s taken a fancy to you<br>And so she lays claim to you. That’s very satisfactory.<br>She’s taken it for granted that you should have the flat —<br>By tomorrow she’ll be sure it was she who proposed it.<br>So I feel pretty confident that, before long,<br>We can put matters onto a permanent basis. |
| COLBY | I must confess, that up to this point<br><br><br>I haven’t been able to feel very settled.<br>And what you’ve had in mind still seems to me<br>Like building my life upon a [[concepts/deception\|deception]].<br>Do you really believe that Lady Elizabeth<br>Can ever accept me as if I was her son? |
| SIR CLAUDE | As if you were her son? If she comes to think of you<br><br><br>As the kind of man that her son would have been —<br>And I believe she will: though I’m perfectly convinced<br>That her son would have been a different type of person —<br><br><br>Then you will become her son, in her eyes. She’s like that.<br><br><br>Why, it wouldn’t surprise me if she came to believe<br>That you really are her son, instead of being mine.<br>She has always lived in a world of make-believe,<br>And the best one can do is to guide her delusions<br>In the right direction. |
| COLBY | It doesn’t seem quite honest.<br><br><br>If we all have to live in a world of make-believe,<br>Is that good for us? Or a kindness to her? |
| SIR CLAUDE | If you haven’t the strength to impose your own terms<br><br><br>Upon life, you must accept the terms it offers you.<br>But tell me first — I’ve a reason for asking —<br>How do you like your work? You don’t find it uncongenial?<br>I’m not changing the subject: I’m coming back to it.<br>You know I’ve deliberately left you [[Alone\|alone]],<br>And so far we’ve discussed only current business,<br>Thinking that you might find it easier<br>To start by a rather formal relationship<br>In adapting yourself to a new situation. |
| COLBY | I’m very grateful to you, for that:<br><br><br>It is indeed a new and strange situation,<br>And nothing about it is real to me yet. |
| SIR CLAUDE | But now I want it to be different. It’s odd, Colby.<br><br><br>I didn’t realise, till you started with me here,<br>That we hardly know each other at all. |
| COLBY | I suppose there hasn’t been the opportunity. |
| SIR CLAUDE | When you were a [[archetypes/child\|child]], you belonged to your aunt,<br><br><br>Or so she made me feel. I never saw you [[Alone\|alone]].<br>And then when I sent you both over to Canada<br>In the war — that was perhaps a mistake,<br>Though it seemed to have such obvious advantages<br>That I had no doubts at the [[motifs/time\|time]] — that’s five years;<br>And then your school, and your military service,<br>And then your absorption in your music … |
| COLBY | You started by asking me how I found this work. |
| SIR CLAUDE | Yes, how do you find it? |
| COLBY | In a way, exhilarating.<br><br><br>To find there is something that I can do<br>So remote from my previous interests.<br>It gives me, in a way, a kind of [[concepts/self\|self]]-confidence<br>I’ve never had before. Yet at the same [[motifs/time\|time]]<br>It’s rather disturbing. I don’t mean the work:<br>I mean, about myself. As if I was becoming<br>A different person. Just as, I suppose,<br>If you learn to speak a foreign language fluently,<br>So that you can think in it — you feel yourself to be<br>Rather a different person when you’re talking it.<br>I’m not at all sure that I like the other person<br>That I feel myself becoming — though he fascinates me.<br>And yet from [[motifs/time\|time]] to [[motifs/time\|time]], when I least expect it,<br>When my mind is cleared and empty, walking in the street<br>Or waking in the [[motifs/night\|night]], then the former person,<br>The person I used to be, returns to take possession:<br>And I am again the disappointed organist,<br>And for a moment the thing I cannot do,<br>The art that I could never excel in,<br>Seems the one thing worth doing, the one thing<br>That I want to do. I have to fight that person. |
| SIR CLAUDE | I understand what you are saying<br><br><br>Much better than you think. It’s my own experience<br>That you are repeating. |
| COLBY | Your own experience? |
| SIR CLAUDE | Yes, I did not want to be a financier. |
| COLBY | What did you want to do? |
| SIR CLAUDE | I wanted to be a potter. |
| COLBY | A potter! |
| SIR CLAUDE | A potter. When I was a boy<br><br><br>I loved to shape things. I loved form and colour<br>And I loved the material that the potter handles.<br>Most people think that a sculptor or a painter<br>Is something more excellent to be than a potter.<br>Most people think of china or porcelain<br>As merely for use, or for decoration —<br>In either case, an inferior art.<br>For me, they are neither ‘use’ nor ‘decoration’ —<br>That is, decoration as a background for living;<br>For me, they are life itself. To be among such things,<br>If it is an escape, is escape into living,<br>Escape from a sordid world to a pure one.<br>Sculpture and painting — I have some good things —<br>But they haven’t this … remoteness I have always longed for.<br>I want a world where the form is the reality,<br>Of which the substantial is only a [[archetypes/shadow\|shadow]].<br>It’s strange. I have never talked of this to anyone.<br>Never until now. Do you feel at all like that<br>When you are [[Alone\|alone]] with your music? |
| COLBY | Just the same.<br><br><br>All the [[motifs/time\|time]] you’ve been speaking, I’ve been translating<br>Into terms of music. But may I ask.<br>With this passion for … ceramics, how did it happen<br>That you never made it your profession? |
| SIR CLAUDE | Family pressure, in the first place.<br><br><br>My father — your grandfather — built up this business<br>Starting from nothing. It was his passion.<br><br><br>He loved it with the same devotion<br>That I gave to clay, and what could be done with it —<br>What I hoped I could do with it. I thought I despised him<br>When I was young. And yet I was in awe of him.<br>I was wrong, in both. I loathed this occupation<br>Until I began to feel my power in it.<br>The life changed me, as it is changing you:<br>It begins as a kind of make-believe<br>And the make-believing makes it real.<br>That’s not the whole story. My father knew I hated it:<br>That was a grief to him. He knew, I am sure,<br>That I cherished for a long [[motifs/time\|time]] a secret reproach:<br>But after his [[motifs/death\|death]], and then it was too late,<br>I knew that he was right. And all my life<br>I have been atoning. To a dead father,<br>Who had always been right. I never understood him.<br>I was too young. And when I was mature enough<br>To understand him, he was not there. |
| COLBY | You’ve still not explained why you came to think<br><br><br>That your father had been right. |
| SIR CLAUDE | Because I came to see<br><br><br>That I should never have become a first-rate potter.<br>I didn’t have it in me. It’s strange, isn’t it,<br>That a man should have a consuming passion<br>To do something for which he lacks the capacity?<br>Could a man be said to have a vocation<br>To be a second-rate potter? To be, at best,<br>A competent copier, possessed by the craving<br>To create, when one is wholly uncreative?<br>I don’t think so. For I came to see<br>That I had always known, at the secret moments,<br>That I didn’t have it in me. There are occasions<br>When I am transported — a different person,<br>Transfigured in the vision of some marvellous creation,<br>And I feel what the man must have felt when he made it.<br>But nothing I made ever gave me that contentment —<br><br><br>That state of utter exhaustion and peace<br>Which comes in dying to give something life …<br>I intend that you shall have a good piano. The best.<br>And when you are [[Alone\|alone]] at your piano, in the evening,<br>I believe you will go through the private door<br>Into the real world, as I do, sometimes. |
| COLBY | Indeed, I have felt, while you’ve been talking,<br><br><br>That it’s my own feelings you have expressed,<br>Although the medium is different. I know<br>I should never have become a great organist,<br>As I aspired to be. I’m not an executant;<br>I’m only a [[archetypes/shadow\|shadow]] of the great composers.<br>Always, when I play to myself,<br>I hear the music I should like to have written,<br>As the composer heard it when it came to him;<br>But when I played before other people<br>I was always conscious that what they heard<br><br><br>Was not what I hear when I play to myself.<br>What I hear is a great musician’s music,<br>What they hear is an inferior rendering.<br>So I’ve given up trying to play to other people:<br>I am only happy when I play to myself. |
| SIR CLAUDE | You shall play to yourself. And as for me,<br><br><br>I keep my pieces in a private room.<br>It isn’t that I don’t want anyone to see them!<br>But when I am [[Alone\|alone]], and look at one thing long enough,<br>I sometimes have that sense of identification<br>With the maker, of which I spoke — an agonising ecstasy<br>Which makes life bearable. It’s all I have.<br>I suppose it takes the place of religion:<br>Just as my wife’s investigations<br>Into what she calls the life of the spirit<br>Are a kind of substitute for religion.<br>I dare say truly religious people —<br>I’ve never known any — can find some [[unity]].<br>Then there are also the men of genius.<br>There are others, it seems to me, who have at best to live<br>In two worlds — each a kind of make-believe.<br>That’s you and me. Some day, perhaps,<br>I will show you my collection. |
| COLBY | Thank you. |
| SIR CLAUDE | And perhaps, some [[motifs/time\|time]], you will let me hear you play.<br><br><br>I shan’t mention it again. I’ll wait until you ask me.<br>Do you understand now what I meant when I spoke<br>Of accepting the terms life imposes upon you<br>Even to the point of accepting … make-believe? |
| COLBY | I think I do. At least, I understand you better<br><br><br>In learning to understand the conditions<br>Which life has imposed upon you. But … something in me<br>Rebels against accepting such conditions.<br>It would be so much simpler if you weren’t my father!<br><br><br>I was struck by what you said, a little while ago,<br>When you spoke of never having understood your father<br>Until it was too late. And you spoke of atonement.<br>Even your failure to understand him,<br>Of which you spoke — that was a relationship<br>Of father and son. It must often happen.<br>And the reconcilement, after his [[motifs/death\|death]],<br>That perfects the relation. You have always been his son<br>And he is still your father. I only wish<br>That I had something to atone for!<br>There’s something lacking, between you and me,<br>That you had, and have, and always will have, with your father.<br>I begin to see how I have always thought of you —<br>As a kind of protector, a generous provider:<br>Rather as a patron than a father —<br>The father who was missing in the years of [[motifs/childhood\|childhood]].<br>Those years have gone forever. The empty years.<br>Oh, I’m terribly sorry to be saying this;<br>But it goes to explain what I said just now<br>About rebelling against the terms<br>That life has imposed. |
| SIR CLAUDE | It’s my own fault.<br><br><br>I was always anxious to avoid the mistakes<br>My father made with me. And yet I seem<br>To have made a greater mistake than he did. |
| COLBY | I know that I’m hurting you and I know<br><br><br>That I hate myself for hurting you. |
| SIR CLAUDE | You mustn’t think of that. |
| COLBY | I’m very grateful for all you’ve done for me;<br><br><br>And I want to do my best to justify your kindness<br>By the work I do. |
| SIR CLAUDE | As my confidential clerk. |
| COLBY | I’m really interested by the work I’m doing<br><br><br>And eager for more. I don’t want my position<br>To be, in any way, a make-believe. |
| SIR CLAUDE | It shan’t be. Meanwhile, we must simply wait to learn<br><br><br>What new conditions life will impose on us.<br>Just when we think we have settled our account<br>Life presents a new one, more difficult to pay.<br>— I shall go now, and sit for a while with my china. |
| COLBY | Excuse me, but I must remind you:<br><br><br>You have that meeting in the [[motifs/city\|City]]<br>Tomorrow morning. You asked me to prepare<br>Some figures for you. I’ve got them here. |
| SIR CLAUDE | Much depends on my wife. Be patient with her, Colby.<br><br><br>— Oh yes, that meeting. We must run through the figures.<br>CURTAIN |

| Chi parla | Battuta |  
|---|---|  
| *(didascalia)* | Lo studio degli affari al primo piano, da [[characters/sir-claude-mulhammer\|SIR CLAUDE MULHAMMER]], a [[settings/london\|Londra]], in [[house\|casa]] sua. Primo pomeriggio. SIR CLAUDE scrive alla scrivania. Entra EGGERSON. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Ah, eccola, Eggerson! Puntuale come sempre.<br><br><br>Mi dispiace doverla far salire fino a [[settings/london\|Londra]],<br>da Joshua Park, per una commissione simile.<br>Ma sa che mia moglie non vorrebbe che ad accoglierla<br>a Northolt ci fosse altri che lei. E non potevo mandare Colby.<br>Non è questo il modo di preparare il loro primo incontro,<br>al suo ritorno dalla Svizzera. |  
| EGGERSON | Impossibile, Sir Claude!<br><br><br>Una situazione delicatissima —<br>il suo primo incontro con il signor Simpkins.<br>Ma ero contento della scusa per venire a [[settings/london\|Londra]]:<br>ho passato la mattinata a far compere! Attrezzi da giardinaggio.<br>Quante cose servono per un [[motifs/garden\|giardino]]!<br>E ho pensato: è il momento giusto per comprare attrezzi nuovi,<br>così da non perdere un istante alla fine dell’[[motifs/winter\|inverno]].<br>E ho trovato una stoffa intonata per la signora E.,<br>che desiderava da un pezzo. Sarà contenta.<br><br><br>Poi ho pranzato al magazzino — hanno un ristorante;<br>un ottimo pranzo, e a buon mercato, per i tempi che corrono.<br>Ma dov’è il signor Simpkins? Sarà qui? |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Stamattina ho dovuto mandarlo alla [[motifs/city\|City]],<br><br><br>ma tornerà, [[concepts/hope\|spero]], prima che lei parta. |  
| EGGERSON | E come se la cava? A meraviglia, ne sono certo,<br><br><br>dato che non ho più saputo nulla dall’ultima [[motifs/time\|volta]] che sono venuto. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | [[motifs/well\|Beh]], naturalmente, Eggerson, lei è insostituibile… |  
| EGGERSON | Oh, Sir Claude, non dovrebbe dirlo!<br><br><br>Il signor Simpkins è molto più qualificato di quanto fossi io<br>per essere il suo segretario privato.<br>Stava prendendo le misure, e in fretta,<br>nel [[motifs/time\|tempo]] in cui abbiamo lavorato insieme.<br>Tutto ciò che gli serve è fiducia. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Ed esperienza.<br><br><br>Con un giovane è necessario qualche assestamento.<br>Ma mi pare che stia afferrando il meccanismo,<br>e credo che cominci a prenderci un vivo interesse. |  
| EGGERSON | E sta superando la sua delusione?<br><br><br>Naturalmente non gliene ho mai parlato:<br>è solo quello che mi ha detto lei. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Riguardo alla sua musica.<br><br><br>Sì, credo di sì. Capisco che cosa prova.<br>È come me, Eggerson. La stessa delusione<br>in forma diversa. Non dimenticherà<br>che la sua grande [[concepts/ambition\|ambizione]] era di fare l’organista,<br>proprio come io non posso dimenticare… non importa.<br>La cosa importante era trovare qualcos’altro<br>che potesse fare, e fare [[motifs/well\|bene]]. E credo che l’abbia trovato,<br>proprio come feci io. Gli parlerò di me.<br>Ma finora l’ho lasciato a se stesso:<br>ho pensato che si sarebbe adattato più in fretta a questa vita<br>se avessimo cominciato su un piano puramente d’affari. |  
| EGGERSON | Senza [[concepts/doubt\|dubbio]] è la cosa migliore. Mentre vive ancora<br><br><br>dalla zia a Teddington, e viene su ogni giorno,<br>proprio come facevo io. E l’appartamentino nella corte?<br>Fra quanto sarà pronto per lui? |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Devono ancora finire le pareti. E poi va ammobiliato.<br><br><br>Sto cercando di trovargli un pianoforte davvero buono. |  
| EGGERSON | Un pianoforte? Sì, sono certo che si sentirà a [[motifs/home\|casa]]<br><br><br>quando avrà un pianoforte. Lei pensa a tutto.<br>Ma se posso permettermi un suggerimento: le cassette da [[motifs/window\|finestra]]!<br>Ha mostrato tanto interesse per il mio [[motifs/garden\|giardino]]<br>che credo dovrebbe avere le cassette da [[motifs/window\|finestra]].<br>Un giorno vorrà un [[motifs/garden\|giardino]] tutto suo. E sì, una vaschetta per gli uccelli! |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Una vaschetta per gli uccelli? Nella corte? A che scopo? |  
| EGGERSON | Mi ha detto che gli piace molto osservare gli uccelli. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Ma non ci saranno [[motifs/birds\|uccelli]] — nessuno che valga la pena osservare. |  
| EGGERSON | Non saprei, Sir Claude. Proprio l’altro giorno<br><br><br>ho letto una lettera sul Times sugli [[motifs/birds\|uccelli]] selvatici avvistati a [[settings/london\|Londra]]:<br><br><br>e sono certo che, se qualcuno li troverà, sarà il signor Simpkins. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | [[motifs/well\|Bene]], lasciamo perdere per ora. Dato che abbiamo un po’ di [[motifs/time\|tempo]]<br><br><br>prima che lei parta per Northolt — l’automobile sarà pronta —<br>pensiamo a che cosa dirà a Lady Elizabeth,<br>tornando dall’aeroporto, a proposito di Colby.<br>Credo che dovrebbe avvertirla<br>di chi incontrerà al suo arrivo. |  
| EGGERSON | Come vorrebbe che affrontassi l’argomento? |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Naturalmente sa che lei desiderava ritirarsi,<br><br><br>dato che ne abbiamo discusso a proposito della sua sostituzione.<br>Ma sa bene che lo considera — [[motifs/well\|beh]], a tutti gli effetti<br>uno di famiglia. |  
| EGGERSON | È un grande complimento. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | E [[motifs/well\|ben]] meritato; ma piuttosto scomodo<br><br><br>quando si tratta di nominare un successore.<br>Rende molto difficile sostituirla.<br>Ritiene di dover avere voce nella scelta;<br>e per di più è convinta che lei, più di chiunque altro,<br>sappia giudicare il carattere meglio di me. |  
| EGGERSON | Oh, non direi, Sir Claude!<br><br><br>Ha troppo rispetto per il suo genio negli affari.<br>Ma è vero che crede di avere ciò che chiama «guida». |  
| SIR CLAUDE | La guida. È peggio che credere nel proprio giudizio:<br><br><br>su quello si potrebbe discutere. Con la guida non si discute.<br>Ma se dovesse sembrare perplessa, o seccata<br>del fatto che ho fatto la nomina durante la sua assenza,<br>dica che lei ha dovuto lasciare per ordine del medico.<br>Si è sempre preoccupata della sua salute,<br>perciò sarà comprensiva. E quanto a Colby —<br>dica che il signor Simpkins aveva ottime referenze,<br>e dica che ho dovuto decidere in fretta<br>perché aveva un’altra offerta molto allettante.<br>Qualcosa del genere. Non ci si dilunghi troppo.<br>E [[concepts/hope\|spero]] proprio che le piaccia subito:<br>se sarà così, finirà di sicuro per credere<br>di averlo scelto lei stessa. A proposito, non dimentichi<br>di farle sapere che è molto musicale.<br>Potrà portarlo ai concerti. Ma non esageri! |  
| EGGERSON | Me ne ricorderò. La musica. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | E a proposito,<br>quanto le ha detto in realtà di lei?<br>Ricorda, le ho chiesto di prepararlo un poco;<br>ci sono cose che lei saprebbe dire meglio di me,<br>e modi in cui potrebbe rassicurarlo<br>meglio di me. Con lei è più a suo agio<br>che con me. |  
| EGGERSON | Oh, non deve dirlo!<br><br><br>Anche se ho fatto del mio meglio per guadagnarmi la sua fiducia.<br>Le ho accennato al suo interesse per la Luce dall’Oriente. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | E l’Apocalisse? E la Sapienza di Atlantide? |  
| EGGERSON | [[motifs/well\|Beh]], a dire il [[concepts/truth\|vero]], Sir Claude, ho solo sfiorato questi argomenti:<br><br><br>sono troppo profondi per me. E ho pensato: il signor Simpkins<br>è molto istruito. Comincerà presto ad afferrarli.<br>No, non gli ho detto molto di Lady Elizabeth.<br>Ma c’è una cosa che vorrei sapere —<br>se non le dispiace — prima di andare a incontrarla.<br>Fra quanto intende… spiegare il signor Simpkins?<br><br><br>Regolarizzare la sua posizione in famiglia?<br>Mi disse che era questa la sua intenzione finale. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Quando — o addirittura se — rivelerò la sua [[concepts/identity\|identità]]<br><br><br>dipende da come lei lo prenderà. Questo pomeriggio<br>saprà soltanto che lei si è finalmente ritirato<br>e che ha un giovane successore,<br>un certo signor [[characters/colby-simpkins\|Colby Simpkins]]. |  
| EGGERSON | Semplicemente il signor Simpkins. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Le ragioni per farlo cominciare durante la sua assenza<br><br><br>sono chiarissime. Ma oltre a quel punto<br>non le ho ancora esposto i miei piani.<br>Il motivo per cui non le ho mai parlato di lui,<br>il motivo per cui lo incontrerà come semplice signor Simpkins,<br>è che lei ha un forte istinto materno… |  
| EGGERSON | Me ne rendo conto. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Che è stato sempre frustrato. |  
| EGGERSON | Sono certo che è stato un dolore per entrambi<br><br><br>non aver mai avuto figli. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Non peggiore, Eggerson,<br><br><br>di quello suo e di sua moglie, per aver avuto un figlio<br>caduto in guerra, e la sua [[motifs/grave\|tomba]] ignota. |  
| EGGERSON | E lei sta pensando senza [[concepts/doubt\|dubbio]] che Lady Elizabeth<br><br><br>sarebbe richiamata al [[archetypes/child\|figlio]] che ha perduto. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | In tutt’altro modo, sì. Si potrebbe dire smarrito,<br>dato che il padre è morto e non c’è modo di rintracciarlo.<br>Sì, pensavo al suo [[archetypes/child\|figlio]] scomparso:<br>date le circostanze, questo potrebbe renderla gelosa.<br>Ho spiegato tutto questo a Colby — al signor Simpkins. |  
| EGGERSON | Capisco che cosa intende. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Prima deve affezionarsi a lui:<br><br><br>e allora, Eggerson, non dispero<br>che, nella convinzione che sia [[archetypes/orphan\|orfano]],<br>voglia che lo adottiamo. |  
| EGGERSON | Adottarlo! Sì, davvero,<br><br><br>quella sarebbe la soluzione. Sì, addirittura ideale. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Sono lieto che concordi. Il suo appoggio sarà utile. |  
| EGGERSON | Sarò felicissimo di raccomandarlo. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Non deve esagerare! Ma la sua approvazione conta.<br><br><br>Sa bene che tiene in altissimo conto il suo parere. |  
| EGGERSON | [[motifs/well\|Beh]], credo che una volta o due, forse…<br><br><br>Ma temo che lei sopravvaluti la mia influenza.<br>Non sono mai riuscito a farle piacere la signorina Angel;<br>si distrae ogni volta che la nomino. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Ma di Lucasta — la signorina Angel — sapeva fin dall’inizio.<br><br><br>Quella era una difficoltà. E ce ne sono altre.<br>Tanto per cominciare, sono tutte e due donne. |  
| EGGERSON | Vero. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Ma non credo che badi molto alla signorina Angel.<br><br><br>Semplicemente non la vede. E la signorina Angel<br>si sposerà presto, immagino. |  
| EGGERSON | Lo [[concepts/hope\|spero]] anch’io. Una sistemazione quanto mai adatta.<br><br><br>Ma mi dica una cosa: se si arrivasse al punto<br>in cui Lady Elizabeth volesse adottarlo —<br>una soluzione ammirevole — che cosa seguirebbe?<br>Le farebbe sapere, allora, che il signor Simpkins<br>è in realtà suo figlio? |  
| SIR CLAUDE | È lì che brancolo nel buio.<br><br><br>Non riesco proprio a immaginare quale sarebbe la sua reazione.<br>C’è molto che non capisco di mia moglie.<br>C’è sempre qualcosa che si ignora<br>di chiunque, per quanto [[motifs/well\|bene]] lo si conosca;<br>e può essere qualcosa della massima importanza.<br>È quando sei sicuro di capire una persona<br>che rischi di sbagliare su di lei nel modo peggiore.<br>A dire il vero, c’è molto che non so<br>di lei, Eggerson, benché abbiamo lavorato insieme<br>per quasi trent’anni. |  
| EGGERSON | Quasi trentuno.<br><br><br>Ma ora che me lo mette così bene,<br>devo ammettere che c’è molto che non capisco<br><br><br>di mia moglie. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | E altrettanto<br><br><br>lei non sa di lei. E altrettanto<br>lei non sa di me — di questo non sono così sicuro!<br>La mia regola è ricordare che non capisco nessuno,<br>ma d’altra parte non essere mai sicuro<br>che gli altri non capiscano me — molto meglio<br>di quanto mi farebbe piacere pensare, forse. |  
| EGGERSON | E devo dedurne<br><br><br>che non è sicuro di capire nemmeno il signor Simpkins? |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Un richiamo opportuno. Forse dovrà ripetermelo.<br><br><br>Ma ormai dovrebbe essere tornato. E allora la lascio.<br>Devo telefonare ad Amsterdam, e forse a [[settings/paris\|Parigi]].<br>Ma quando lei tornerà con Lady Elizabeth<br>sarò pronto ad aspettarvi per presentarlo. |  
| *(didascalia)* | [Entra [[characters/colby-simpkins\|COLBY SIMPKINS]] con una cartella] |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Ah, Colby, stavo appunto dicendo a Eggerson<br><br><br>che era [[motifs/time\|ora]] che tornasse. La mattinata è andata bene? |  
| COLBY | Ho quel che voleva, Sir Claude. Buon pomeriggio,<br><br><br>signor Eggerson. Temevo di non trovarla più. |  
| EGGERSON | Parto fra mezz’ora, signor Simpkins. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Ora vi lascio. Ma quando Eggerson tornerà<br><br><br>con Lady Elizabeth, vi raggiungerò. |  
| *(didascalia)* | [Esce SIR CLAUDE] |  
| COLBY | Sono contento che non debba andarsene subito.<br><br><br>Sono piuttosto nervoso per questo incontro.<br>Mi ha detto pochissimo di Lady Elizabeth,<br>e Sir Claude stesso non mi ha detto molto:<br>così non ho idea di come dovrei comportarmi.<br>B. Kaghan mi ha raccontato qualcosa di lei,<br>ma è piuttosto allarmante. |  
| EGGERSON | Il signor Kaghan è prevenuto.<br><br><br>Non è mai andato d’accordo con Lady Elizabeth.<br>Non gli dia retta. Capisce Sir Claude,<br>e a Sir Claude è sempre stato molto riconoscente,<br>come è giusto che sia. Sir Claude lo scelse<br>e gli diede l’avvio. E lui ne ha tratto il massimo —<br>questo glielo riconosco. Un esempio incoraggiante<br>per lei, signor Simpkins. Sarà una potenza nella [[motifs/city\|City]]!<br>E ha un cuore d’oro. Ma, senza girarci intorno,<br>è un diamante piuttosto grezzo. Modi assai disinvolti;<br>e Lady Elizabeth non gli ha mai preso simpatia.<br>Ma lei, signor Simpkins, è tutt’altra cosa. |  
| COLBY | Non capisco perché dovrebbe essere così diverso.<br><br><br>B. Kaghan mi piace. L’ho trovato molto disponibile<br>e ottima compagnia anche fuori dagli affari. |  
| EGGERSON | Oh sì, il signor Kaghan è ottima compagnia.<br><br><br>A volte mi fa ridere. E io non rido facilmente.<br>È un bel tipo di spiritoso. Anzi, la signora E.<br>a volte mi dice: «Eggerson, perché non mi fai ridere<br>come faceva B. Kaghan?» L’ha visto una volta sola;<br>ma sa che cosa fece? Cominciò a chiamarla Muriel —<br>nei primi dieci minuti! Io ero inorridito.<br>Ma a lei in realtà piacque. Muriel è il suo nome.<br><br><br>Ha un certo modo con le signore, sa.<br>Ma con Lady Elizabeth non ha avuto altrettanto successo.<br>Una volta lei lo definì «senza distinzione»;<br>ma con lei, come dicevo, sarà tutt’altra cosa.<br>Vedrà subito che lei è un uomo di cultura;<br>e poi lei è molto musicale. |  
| COLBY | Grazie dell’avvertimento! |  
| EGGERSON | Perciò, se non le dispiace, accennerò subito<br><br><br>al fatto che lei è un musicista. |  
| COLBY | Starò in guardia. |  
| EGGERSON | La sua musica sarà di certo una gran risorsa<br><br><br>con Lady Elizabeth. Gliela invidio.<br>Io ho sempre cantato nel nostro coro parrocchiale<br>e alla funzione dei [[carol\|canti natalizi]]. Ma vorrei essere musicale. |  
| COLBY | Continuo a non sentirmi molto [[motifs/well\|ben]] preparato a incontrarla. |  
| *(didascalia)* | [Un bussare fragoroso. Entra B. KAGHAN] |  
| *(didascalia)* | Entra B. Kaghan. Ciao Colby! |  
| KAGHAN | E ciao Eggers! Sono contento di trovarla qui.<br>È una fortuna per Colby. |  
| EGGERSON | E perché mai, signor Kaghan? |  
| KAGHAN | Perché c’è Lucasta con me! La solita catastrofe.<br><br><br>È venuta a scucire un po’ di contante dalla cassa.<br>In bancarotta di nuovo! Così ho pensato di portarla<br>e di salire avanti io, per attutire il colpo a Colby.<br>Ma dato che c’è lei, Eggers, posso rilassarmi.<br>Mi godrò la partita dalla linea laterale. |  
| *(didascalia)* | [Entra LUCASTA ANGEL] |  
| LUCASTA | Eggy, ho perso il posto! |  
| EGGERSON | Di nuovo, signorina Angel? |  
| LUCASTA | Sì, di nuovo! E gli sta bene! |  
| EGGERSON | È stata, presumo, ostinatamente in ritardo. |  
| LUCASTA | Si sbaglia, Eggy. È pura ingiustizia.<br><br><br>Per due mesi ho archiviato quelle carte<br>che nessuno voleva mai — almeno, fino a ieri.<br>Poi, giusto per sfortuna, il capo volle una lettera<br>e io non riuscii a trovarla. E allora si insospettì<br>e chiese cose che di sicuro non gli servivano —<br>solo per far dispetto! E non ne trovai neanche una.<br>Ma sono tutte archiviate da qualche parte, ne sono certa, quindi che problema c’è?<br>Ma chi è questo, Eggy? È [[characters/colby-simpkins\|Colby Simpkins]]?<br>Presentatemelo, l’uno o l’altro di voi. |  
| EGGERSON | Il signor Simpkins, la signorina Angel. Come sa, signorina Angel,<br><br><br>il signor Simpkins ha preso il mio posto. |  
| LUCASTA | E sa che io sono uno dei suoi compiti?<br><br><br>L’ha preparato a prendersi cura anche di me?<br><br><br>Lo sapeva, Colby? Io sono Lucasta.<br>Solo Eggy mi chiama signorina Angel,<br>tanto per infastidirmi. Non trova<br>che Lucasta mi si addica di più? |  
| COLBY | Sono certo che le si addicono entrambi. |  
| LUCASTA | Snobbata di nuovo! Immagino di essermela cercata.<br><br><br>Ecco che cosa comporta la maledizione di un nome come Angel.<br>Sto pensando di cambiarlo. Ma, Colby,<br>lo sa che sono una delle sue responsabilità? |  
| COLBY | No, temo di non saperlo. |  
| EGGERSON | Non deve cederle, signor Simpkins.<br>Io non lo faccio mai. Dico sempre<br>che se si dà alla signorina Angel un dito<br>lei si prende un’elle. |  
| LUCASTA | Elle come Lucasta.<br><br><br>Continui, Eggy. Non gli dia retta, Colby.<br>Colby, lei è sposato? |  
| COLBY | No, non sono sposato. |  
| LUCASTA | Allora non mi dispiace farmi vedere con lei in pubblico.<br><br><br>Può portarmi fuori a cena. Una ragazza che lavora come me<br>ha spesso una gran fame — vivendo con un’inezia —<br>a cuocersi una salsiccia sul fornello a gas… |  
| EGGERSON | Non deve credere a una sola parola di quel che dice. |  
| LUCASTA | Il signor Simpkins crederà a tutto quello che dico,<br><br><br>signor Eggerson. E so che sarà gentile con me<br><br><br>quando lei sarà fuori dai piedi. Perché non lo lascia parlare?<br>Eggy in fondo è quasi umano, Colby.<br>È solo che ha un terrore folle della signora Eggerson;<br>ecco perché non mi ha mai invitata a pranzo. |  
| EGGERSON | Lasciamo fuori la signora Eggerson da tutto questo, signorina Angel. |  
| LUCASTA | È quello che dice sempre, Colby,<br><br><br>quando nomino la signora Eggerson. Abbocca ogni volta.<br>B.! Che cosa hai raccontato di me a Colby? |  
| KAGHAN | È inutile raccontare di te a chicchessia:<br><br><br>nessuno crederebbe alla tua esistenza<br>finché non ti incontra. Colby vacilla ancora.<br>Sarà una mia responsabilità,<br>come tuo fidanzato, proteggere Colby da te.<br>Ma prima affrontiamo la crisi finanziaria. |  
| LUCASTA | Sì, Eggy, vuole dare lei la triste notizia a Claude?<br><br><br>Intanto, dovrà saccheggiare la cassa per me. Muoio di fame. |  
| KAGHAN | Le ho appena offerto il pranzo. Il problema con Lucasta<br><br><br>è come tenerla sfamata fra un pasto e l’altro. |  
| LUCASTA | B., sei una bestia. Ho un appetito piccolissimo.<br><br><br>Ma il punto è che sono senza un soldo. |  
| KAGHAN | Ha avuto una settimana di stipendio in luogo del preavviso. |  
| LUCASTA | B., ricordati che sei mio fidanzato solo in prova.<br><br><br>Posso avere un po’ di denaro, Eggy? |  
| EGGERSON | Non sono più io a occuparmene<br><br><br>e quell’incombenza non è passata al signor Simpkins:<br><br><br>Sir Claude intende trattare queste faccende di persona.<br>Dovrà chiedere a Sir Claude. Ma gliene parlerò io<br>al ritorno da Northolt. |  
| LUCASTA | Va a incontrare Lizzie? |  
| EGGERSON | Vado a incontrare Lady Elizabeth a Northolt. |  
| LUCASTA | [[motifs/well\|Beh]], non ho intenzione di essere sulla scena quando arriva. |  
| KAGHAN | E io non ho intenzione di lasciarti con Colby.<br><br><br>Ne ha avuto abbastanza per un giorno solo. Segui il mio consiglio, Colby.<br>Non concedere mai a Lucasta il minimo vantaggio<br>o lei lo sfrutterà. Con lei bisogna essere duri;<br>è dura come i chiodi. Ora te la tolgo dai piedi.<br>Ti farò vedere come si fa. Andiamo, Lucasta,<br>farò le cose in grande e ti porterò fuori a prendere il tè. |  
| LUCASTA | Muoio dalla voglia del tè. La tensione di questa crisi<br><br><br>è stata troppo per me. Un’altra [[motifs/time\|volta]], Colby.<br>La chiamerò, e mi lascerò portare fuori a pranzo. |  
| *(didascalia)* | [Esce LUCASTA] |  
| KAGHAN | Con calma, Colby. Ci farai l’abitudine. |  
| *(didascalia)* | [Esce KAGHAN] |  
| COLBY | Egg… Signor Eggerson! |  
| EGGERSON | Sì, signor Simpkins? |  
| COLBY | Lei mi sembra sano di mente. E credo di esserlo anch’io. |  
| EGGERSON | Non ho [[concepts/doubt\|dubbi]] né sull’uno né sull’altro punto, nessunissimo. |  
| COLBY | E anche B. Kaghan mi è sempre parso sano di mente. |  
| EGGERSON | Lo definirei il ritratto stesso della [[Sanity\|sanità mentale]]. |  
| COLBY | Ma di sicuro non mi aveva avvertito riguardo alla signorina Angel.<br><br><br>Come la mettiamo con lei? |  
| EGGERSON | Oh, la signorina Angel.<br><br><br>È piuttosto sventata. Ma ha buon cuore. |  
| COLBY | Ma davvero si rivolge a [[characters/sir-claude-mulhammer\|Sir Claude Mulhammer]]<br><br><br>chiamandolo Claude? In faccia? |  
| EGGERSON | Proprio così. |  
| COLBY | E chiama Lady Elizabeth «Lizzie»? |  
| EGGERSON | [[motifs/well\|Beh]], non in sua presenza. Non quando c’ero io.<br><br><br>No, non credo che lo farebbe. Ma la chiama Lizzie,<br>a volte, parlando con Sir Claude. E sa una cosa?<br>Credo che a lui la faccenda diverta. |  
| COLBY | [[motifs/well\|Beh]], forse divertirà anche me.<br><br><br>Ma mi ha fatto girare la testa — tutti quei nomi di battesimo<br>la prima [[motifs/time\|volta]] che l’ho incontrata. Non ci sono abituato. |  
| EGGERSON | Ci farà presto l’abitudine. Chiamerà me Eggers<br><br><br>prima di accorgersene! |  
| COLBY | Non me ne [[concepts/wonder\|stupirei]].<br><br><br>Ci sono quasi arrivato, poco fa.<br>Allora sarei stato certo di aver perso la ragione:<br>la sua influenza è addirittura spaventosa.<br>Ma mi parli di Lu… della signorina Angel:<br>che rapporto ha con questa casa? |  
| EGGERSON | [[motifs/well\|Beh]]. Una specie di rapporto fiduciario.<br><br><br>No, non credo che sia proprio il termine giusto.<br>Non ha denaro suo, come avrà intuito;<br>ma credo che suo padre fosse amico di Sir Claude,<br>e lui si è assunto la responsabilità di lei.<br>In ogni caso si è comportato da padre —<br>un uomo molto generoso, Sir Claude.<br>A dire il [[concepts/truth\|vero]], lei è una specie di spina nel suo fianco:<br>perde sempre il posto, perché non sa tenerselo.<br>Lui le passa un assegno — davvero più che adeguato,<br>anche se lei è sempre indebitata. Ma non deve preoccuparsi<br>per lei, signor Simpkins. Sposerà il signor Kaghan<br>alla fine. È un uomo che ottiene ciò che vuole,<br>e credo che sappia tenerla a bada. Se qualcuno può. |  
| COLBY | Ma è probabile che sia una seccatura? |  
| EGGERSON | Non se non le si dà corda.<br><br><br>Io non gliel’ho mai data. |  
| COLBY | Ma lei ha la signora Eggerson. |  
| EGGERSON | Sì, è una gran protezione. E ho il mio [[motifs/garden\|giardino]]<br><br><br>a proteggermi dalla signora E. È la mia battuta. |  
| COLBY | [[motifs/well\|Beh]], non ho mai conosciuto nessuno come la signorina Angel. |  
| EGGERSON | Ci farà l’abitudine, signor Simpkins.<br><br><br>Il [[motifs/time\|tempo]] fa miracoli, è quel che dico sempre.<br>Ma non credo che dovrà vederla molto:<br>quella responsabilità non pesa sulle sue spalle.<br>Lady Elizabeth, invece, è un altro discorso. |  
| COLBY | Almeno non credo che Lady Elizabeth<br><br><br>possa essere insolita quanto la signorina Angel. |  
| EGGERSON | Oh sì, signor Simpkins, molto più insolita. |  
| COLBY | Oh! |  
| EGGERSON | [[motifs/well\|Beh]], come le dicevo, lei è davvero una gran dama,<br><br><br>una grande dame, come dicono i francesi.<br>È questo che Sir Claude ammira in lei.<br>Una volta mi disse, in un momento di confidenza —<br>era appena tornato da un pranzo ufficiale —<br>«Eggerson», disse, «volevo una signora,<br>e sono perfettamente soddisfatto dell’affare».<br>È vero, naturalmente, che le sue parentele<br>a volte sono state utili. Ma lui non ci pensò:<br>non è di animo meschino — anche se nulla gli sfugge.<br>E che cuore generoso! È piuttosto socialista.<br>Io, per parte mia, sono un conservatore convinto. |  
| COLBY | Ma Lady Elizabeth è molto insolita<br><br><br>anche per altri versi, oltre che per essere una gran dama? |  
| EGGERSON | Ma sì, certo, devo ammettere che lo è.<br><br><br>Quasi tutte le sue stranezze sono del tutto innocue.<br>Ci farà presto l’abitudine. È quel che disse Sir Claude:<br>«[[Humour\|Assecondala]], Eggerson», disse, «[[Humour\|assecondala]]».<br>Ma c’è un tratto che credo di aver già sfiorato:<br>è molto distratta. |  
| COLBY | [[concepts/hope\|Spero]] che non intenda<br><br><br>che ha dei vuoti di [[motifs/memory\|memoria]]. |  
| EGGERSON | Non intendevo questo.<br><br><br>No. Non ha molta [[motifs/memory\|memoria]] da perdere,<br>anche se a volte ricorda quando meno te lo aspetti.<br>Ma dimentica le cose. E le piace viaggiare,<br>soprattutto per la salute. E quando è all’estero<br>è capace di comprare una [[house\|casa]]. E poi se ne va<br>e se ne dimentica del tutto. Può essere complicato<br>e molto costoso. Ne ho vissute delle belle!<br>Ricordo che tanto tempo fa dicevo alla signora E.,<br>quando avevamo comprato la nostra [[house\|casa]] a Joshua Park<br>(con un mutuo, s’intende): «ora che ci siamo sistemati<br>tutto il viaggiare che voglio è arrivare alla [[motifs/city\|City]]<br><br><br>e tornare a Joshua Park la sera,<br>e una volta l’anno la vacanza a Dawlish».<br>E pensare che era solo l’inizio dei miei viaggi!<br>È stato un privilegio davvero insolito<br>vedere tanta Europa quanta ne ho vista,<br>tirando Lady Elizabeth fuori dai suoi guai. |  
| COLBY | Forse non arriverà nemmeno con questo aereo. |  
| EGGERSON | Oh, potrebbe succedere. A volte si perde,<br>o perde il biglietto, o perfino il passaporto.<br>Ma non attraversiamo i ponti<br>prima di arrivarci. È quel che dico sempre.<br><br><br>E sono certo che le piacerà. È una tale signora!<br><br><br>E per di più ha buon cuore. |  
| COLBY | Sembra che tutti abbiano buon cuore.<br><br><br>Ma c’è una cosa in cui credo, signor Eggerson:<br>che lei abbia un cuore gentile. E sono convinto<br><br><br>che riesca sempre a pensare il meglio di chiunque. |  
| EGGERSON | Vedrà che ho ragione, glielo assicuro. |

Il suo viso mi è familiare. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Questo giovanotto è il successore di Eggerson.<br><br><br>Sai che Eggerson aveva intenzione di ritirarsi… |  
| EGGERSON | Per ordine del medico, Lady Elizabeth:<br><br><br>il dottore è stato molto perentorio… |

| SIR CLAUDE | Non volevo disturbarti, durante la tua cura… |

| SIR CLAUDE | [[characters/colby-simpkins\|Colby Simpkins]].<br><br><br>LADY ELIZABETH [contando sulle dita]. Tredici lettere. Ma ha l’aria piuttosto gracile.<br>Devo darle delle lezioni sull’arte della salute.<br>Dov’è la sua [[motifs/home\|casa]], signor Colby? |  
| COLBY | Simpkins. |  
| EGGERSON | Il signor [[characters/colby-simpkins\|Colby Simpkins]]. |

| LADY ELIZABETH | Ma tutto nei colori sbagliati, ne sono certa. Mio marito<br><br><br>non comprende l’importanza del colore<br>per la nostra vita spirituale, signor Colby.<br>E nemmeno, mi duole dirlo, Eggerson.<br>Che colore avete scelto, voi due? |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Pensavo che un giallo primula sarebbe stato allegro. |

Ora vado a riposare.<br>In un vagone letto è del tutto impossibile<br>trovare la propria ora di quiete. Un’ora di quiete al giorno<br>è essenziale, dice il dottor Rebmann. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Rebmann? Credevo si trattasse di un dottor Leroux. |  
| LADY ELIZABETH | Il dottor Leroux è a Losanna.<br><br><br>Io sono stata a Zurigo, dal dottor Rebmann. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Ma dovevi andare dal dottor Leroux<br><br><br>a Losanna. Che cosa ti ha fatto andare a Zurigo? |

| SIR CLAUDE | Così proseguisti per Zurigo.<br><br><br>Ma credevo che il dottore di Losanna insegnasse il controllo della mente. |  
| LADY ELIZABETH | No, Claude, lui insegna soltanto il controllo del pensiero.<br><br><br>Il controllo della mente è tutt’altra faccenda:<br>è più avanzato. Ma te ne ho scritto per filo e per segno. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | È vero, mi hai mandato delle cartoline da Zurigo;<br><br><br>ma sai che non riesco a decifrare la tua calligrafia.<br>Mi piace ricevere le cartoline, giusto per sapere dove sei<br>leggendo il timbro postale. |  
| LADY ELIZABETH | Ma Claude, sono contenta di vedere<br><br><br>che hai seguito il mio consiglio. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Il tuo consiglio? Riguardo a che cosa? |  
| LADY ELIZABETH | Di assumere il signor Colby. Sono davvero costernata!<br><br><br>Non è il primo segno che ho notato<br>del cedimento della tua [[motifs/memory\|memoria]]. Devo persuaderti<br>a fare un ciclo di cure dal dottor Rebmann —<br>no, al tuo stadio, credo, dal dottor Leroux.<br>Non ricordi? Dissi prima di partire:<br>«Fidati per una volta della mia guida e assumi quel giovanotto».<br>[[motifs/well\|Ebbene]], quello era il signor Colby. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Oh, capisco.<br><br><br>Sì, adesso comincio a ricordare.<br>Devo aver agito seguendo la tua guida. |

| SIR CLAUDE | Sì, va’ a riposare.<br><br><br>Io sono nel mezzo di certi affari col signor… |  
| LADY ELIZABETH | Colby! |

| EGGERSON | Straordinario, vero! |

| COLBY | Grazie mille, lo farò. È rassicurante<br><br><br>sapere di averla sempre alle spalle<br>se mi caccio nei guai. Ma [[concepts/hope\|spero]]<br>di non doverla disturbare spesso. |

| SIR CLAUDE | Arrivederci, e grazie, Eggerson. |  
| EGGERSON | Buona giornata, Sir Claude. Buona giornata, signor Simpkins. |

| SIR CLAUDE | [[motifs/well\|Bene]], Colby! Finora l’ho chiamata signor Simpkins<br><br><br>in pubblico, per prudenza.<br>Come avevamo stabilito. Ma dopo due mesi —<br>e dato che mia moglie insiste nel chiamarla signor Colby —<br>comincerò a chiamarla Colby davanti a tutti. |  
| COLBY | Sono certo che sarà più facile per entrambi. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Il suo arrivo improvviso è stato molto sconcertante:<br><br><br>come avrà capito, una cosa simile non era mai accaduta.<br>Così l’incontro non è andato proprio come avevo previsto;<br>eppure credo che sia stato tutto per il meglio.<br>È andato molto [[motifs/well\|bene]]. | COLBY | Ha davvero creduto<br><br><br>di avermi già visto? |
