È molto soddisfacente.<br>Ha dato per scontato che lei debba avere l’appartamento —<br>domani sarà certa di averlo proposto lei.<br>Perciò sono abbastanza fiducioso che, presto,<br>potremo mettere le cose su basi permanenti. |  
| COLBY | Devo confessare che, fino a questo punto,<br><br><br>non sono riuscito a sentirmi molto tranquillo.<br>E quel che lei ha in mente mi sembra ancora<br>come costruire la mia vita su un [[concepts/deception\|inganno]].<br>Crede davvero che Lady Elizabeth<br>possa mai accettarmi come se fossi suo figlio? |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Come se fosse suo figlio? Se arriverà a pensare a lei<br><br><br>come al tipo d’uomo che sarebbe stato suo figlio —<br>e credo che lo farà: sebbene io sia perfettamente convinto<br>che suo figlio sarebbe stato una persona di tutt’altro tipo —<br><br><br>allora lei diventerà suo figlio, ai suoi occhi. È fatta così.<br><br><br>Anzi, non mi sorprenderebbe se arrivasse a credere<br>che lei è davvero suo figlio, invece che mio.<br>Ha sempre vissuto in un mondo di finzione,<br>e il meglio che si possa fare è guidare le sue illusioni<br>nella direzione giusta. |  
| COLBY | Non mi pare del tutto onesto.<br><br><br>Se dobbiamo tutti vivere in un mondo di finzione,<br>è un bene per noi? O una gentilezza verso di lei? |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Se non si ha la forza di imporre alla vita le proprie condizioni,<br><br><br>bisogna accettare quelle che essa offre.<br>Ma mi dica prima — ho una ragione per chiederlo —<br>le piace il suo lavoro? Non lo trova sgradevole?<br>Non sto cambiando argomento: ci sto tornando.<br>Sa che l’ho lasciata deliberatamente [[Alone\|solo]],<br>e finora abbiamo discusso solo di affari correnti,<br>pensando che le sarebbe stato più facile<br>cominciare con un rapporto piuttosto formale<br>nell’adattarsi a una situazione nuova. |  
| COLBY | Le sono molto grato di questo:<br><br><br>è davvero una situazione nuova e strana,<br>e nulla di essa mi è ancora reale. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Ma ora voglio che sia diverso. È curioso, Colby.<br><br><br>Non mi ero reso conto, finché lei non è entrato qui con me,<br>che ci conosciamo a malapena. |  
| COLBY | Immagino che non ce ne sia stata l’occasione. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Quando lei era [[archetypes/child\|bambino]], apparteneva a sua zia,<br><br><br>o almeno così me lo faceva sentire lei. Non l’ho mai visto [[Alone\|solo]].<br>E poi, quando vi mandai tutti e due in Canada<br>durante la guerra — forse fu un errore,<br>anche se sembrava avere vantaggi così evidenti<br>che al [[motifs/time\|momento]] non ebbi dubbi — sono cinque anni;<br>e poi la sua scuola, e il servizio militare,<br>e poi il suo assorbimento nella musica… |  
| COLBY | Aveva cominciato chiedendomi come trovo questo lavoro. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Sì, come lo trova? |  
| COLBY | In un certo senso, esaltante.<br><br><br>Scoprire che c’è qualcosa che so fare<br>e così remoto dai miei interessi di prima.<br>Mi dà, in un certo senso, una specie di fiducia in [[concepts/self\|me stesso]]<br>che non ho mai avuto. Eppure al [[motifs/time\|tempo]] stesso<br>è piuttosto inquietante. Non intendo il lavoro:<br>intendo, riguardo a me stesso. Come se stessi diventando<br>una persona diversa. Proprio come, immagino,<br>quando si impara a parlare correntemente una lingua straniera,<br>tanto da poterci pensare — ci si sente<br>una persona un po’ diversa mentre la si parla.<br>Non sono affatto sicuro che mi piaccia l’altra persona<br>che sento di stare diventando — benché mi affascini.<br>Eppure di [[motifs/time\|tempo]] in [[motifs/time\|tempo]], quando meno me l’aspetto,<br>quando la mente si sgombra e si svuota, camminando per strada<br>o svegliandomi nella [[motifs/night\|notte]], allora la persona di prima,<br>quella che ero, torna a prendere possesso di me:<br>e sono di nuovo l’organista deluso,<br>e per un attimo la cosa che non so fare,<br>l’arte in cui non potrei mai eccellere,<br>mi pare l’unica cosa che valga la pena di fare, l’unica cosa<br>che io voglia fare. Devo combattere quella persona. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Capisco quel che sta dicendo<br><br><br>molto meglio di quanto lei creda. È la mia stessa esperienza<br>che lei sta ripetendo. |  
| COLBY | La sua esperienza? |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Sì, io non volevo fare il finanziere. |  
| COLBY | Che cosa voleva fare? |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Volevo fare il ceramista. |  
| COLBY | Il ceramista! |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Il ceramista. Quando ero ragazzo<br><br><br>amavo dar forma alle cose. Amavo la forma e il colore<br>e amavo la materia che il ceramista maneggia.<br>Quasi tutti pensano che uno scultore o un pittore<br>sia qualcosa di più eccellente di un ceramista.<br>Quasi tutti pensano alla ceramica o alla porcellana<br>come a semplice uso, o decorazione —<br>in entrambi i casi, un’arte inferiore.<br>Per me non sono né «uso» né «decorazione» —<br>cioè decorazione come sfondo del vivere;<br>per me sono la vita stessa. Stare fra tali cose,<br>se è una fuga, è una fuga dentro il vivere,<br>fuga da un mondo sordido verso un mondo puro.<br>La scultura e la pittura — ne ho dei bei pezzi —<br>ma non hanno quella… lontananza che ho sempre desiderato.<br>Voglio un mondo dove la forma sia la realtà,<br>di cui il sostanziale non è che l’[[archetypes/shadow\|ombra]].<br>È strano. Non ne ho mai parlato con nessuno.<br>Mai fino ad ora. Prova anche lei qualcosa di simile<br>quando è [[Alone\|solo]] con la sua musica? |  
| COLBY | Esattamente lo stesso.<br><br><br>Per tutto il [[motifs/time\|tempo]] che ha parlato, ho tradotto<br>in termini di musica. Ma posso chiederle:<br>con questa passione per… la ceramica, come è successo<br>che non ne ha fatto la sua professione? |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Le pressioni della famiglia, in primo luogo.<br><br><br>Mio padre — suo nonno — costruì questa azienda<br>partendo dal nulla. Era la sua passione.<br><br><br>La amava con la stessa devozione<br>che io davo all’argilla, e a ciò che se ne poteva ricavare —<br>a ciò che speravo di ricavarne. Credevo di disprezzarlo<br>quando ero giovane. Eppure ne avevo soggezione.<br>Mi sbagliavo, in entrambe le cose. Detestavo questo mestiere<br>finché non cominciai a sentirci la mia forza.<br>Quella vita mi cambiò, come sta cambiando lei:<br>comincia come una specie di finzione<br>e il fingere la rende reale.<br>Non è tutta la storia. Mio padre sapeva che lo odiavo:<br>e questo fu un dolore per lui. Sapeva, ne sono certo,<br>che per lungo [[motifs/time\|tempo]] coltivai un rimprovero segreto:<br>ma dopo la sua [[motifs/death\|morte]], e allora era troppo tardi,<br>capii che aveva ragione. E per tutta la vita<br>ho espiato. Verso un padre morto,<br>che aveva sempre avuto ragione. Non l’ho mai capito.<br>Ero troppo giovane. E quando fui abbastanza maturo<br>per capirlo, lui non c’era più. |  
| COLBY | Non ha ancora spiegato perché arrivò a pensare<br><br><br>che suo padre avesse avuto ragione. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Perché arrivai a capire<br><br><br>che non sarei mai diventato un ceramista di prim’ordine.<br>Non l’avevo dentro. È strano, no,<br>che un uomo possa avere una passione divorante<br>per qualcosa di cui gli manca la capacità?<br>Si potrebbe dire che un uomo abbia la vocazione<br>di essere un ceramista di second’ordine? Di essere, nel migliore dei casi,<br>un abile copista, posseduto dalla brama<br>di creare, quando si è del tutto privi di creatività?<br>Non credo. Perché arrivai a vedere<br>di aver sempre saputo, nei momenti segreti,<br>che non l’avevo dentro. Ci sono occasioni<br>in cui vengo rapito — una persona diversa,<br>trasfigurata nella visione di qualche meravigliosa creazione,<br>e sento ciò che dev’essere stato il sentire di chi la fece.<br>Ma nulla di ciò che ho fatto mi ha mai dato quella contentezza —<br><br><br>quello stato di estenuazione assoluta e di pace<br>che viene dal morire per dare vita a qualcosa…<br>Intendo che lei abbia un buon pianoforte. Il migliore.<br>E quando sarà [[Alone\|solo]] al suo pianoforte, la sera,<br>credo che varcherà la porta segreta<br>ed entrerà nel mondo reale, come faccio io, a volte. |  
| COLBY | In verità, mentre lei parlava, ho sentito<br><br><br>che erano i miei sentimenti quelli che esprimeva,<br>benché il mezzo sia diverso. So<br>che non sarei mai diventato un grande organista,<br>come aspiravo a essere. Non sono un esecutore;<br>sono soltanto un’[[archetypes/shadow\|ombra]] dei grandi compositori.<br>Sempre, quando suono per me,<br>sento la musica che avrei voluto scrivere,<br>come il compositore la sentì quando gli venne;<br>ma quando suonavo davanti ad altri<br>ero sempre consapevole che ciò che udivano<br><br><br>non era ciò che odo io quando suono per me.<br>Ciò che odo è la musica di un grande musicista,<br>ciò che odono loro è un’esecuzione inferiore.<br>Così ho rinunciato a suonare per gli altri:<br>sono felice solo quando suono per me. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Suonerà per sé. E quanto a me,<br><br><br>tengo i miei pezzi in una stanza privata.<br>Non è che non voglia che nessuno li veda!<br>Ma quando sono [[Alone\|solo]], e guardo a lungo una cosa sola,<br>a volte provo quel senso di identificazione<br>con chi l’ha fatta, di cui parlavo — un’estasi straziante<br>che rende la vita sopportabile. È tutto ciò che ho.<br>Immagino che prenda il posto della religione:<br>proprio come le indagini di mia moglie<br>in quella che lei chiama la vita dello spirito<br>sono una specie di surrogato della religione.<br>Suppongo che le persone davvero religiose —<br>non ne ho mai conosciute — sappiano trovare una qualche [[unity\|unità]].<br>Poi ci sono anche gli uomini di genio.<br>Ce ne sono altri, mi pare, che nel migliore dei casi devono vivere<br>in due mondi — ciascuno una specie di finzione.<br>Siamo lei e io. Un giorno, forse,<br>le mostrerò la mia collezione. |  
| COLBY | Grazie. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | E forse, col [[motifs/time\|tempo]], mi lascerà sentire come suona.<br><br><br>Non ne parlerò più. Aspetterò che me lo chieda lei.<br>Capisce adesso che cosa intendevo quando parlavo<br>di accettare le condizioni che la vita le impone,<br>fino al punto di accettare… la finzione? |  
| COLBY | Credo di sì. Almeno, la capisco meglio<br><br><br>imparando a capire le condizioni<br>che la vita ha imposto a lei. Ma… qualcosa in me<br>si ribella ad accettare condizioni simili.<br>Sarebbe tutto molto più semplice se lei non fosse mio padre!<br><br><br>Mi ha colpito quel che ha detto poco fa,<br>quando ha parlato di non aver mai capito suo padre<br>finché non fu troppo tardi. E ha parlato di espiazione.<br>Perfino il suo non riuscire a capirlo,<br>di cui ha parlato — anche quello era un rapporto<br>di padre e figlio. Dev’essere una cosa frequente.<br>E la riconciliazione, dopo la sua [[motifs/death\|morte]],<br>che porta a compimento il rapporto. Lei è sempre stato suo figlio<br>e lui è ancora suo padre. Vorrei soltanto<br>avere qualcosa da espiare!<br>C’è qualcosa che manca, fra lei e me,<br>e che lei ha avuto, e ha, e sempre avrà, con suo padre.<br>Comincio a vedere come l’ho sempre pensata —<br>come una specie di protettore, un generoso benefattore:<br>più come un mecenate che come un padre —<br>il padre che è mancato negli anni dell’[[motifs/childhood\|infanzia]].<br>Quegli anni se ne sono andati per sempre. Gli anni vuoti.<br>Oh, mi dispiace terribilmente dire questo;<br>ma serve a spiegare quel che ho detto poco fa<br>sul ribellarmi alle condizioni<br>che la vita ha imposto. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | È colpa mia.<br><br><br>Ho sempre voluto evitare gli errori<br>che mio padre fece con me. Eppure mi pare<br>di aver commesso un errore più grande del suo. |  
| COLBY | So che le sto facendo male e so<br><br><br>che mi odio per il male che le faccio. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Non deve pensarci. |  
| COLBY | Le sono molto grato di tutto quello che ha fatto per me;<br><br><br>e voglio fare del mio meglio per giustificare la sua bontà<br>con il lavoro che faccio. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Come mio segretario privato. |  
| COLBY | Il lavoro che faccio mi interessa davvero<br><br><br>e ne desidero altro. Non voglio che la mia posizione<br>sia, in nessun modo, una finzione. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Non lo sarà. Intanto dobbiamo semplicemente aspettare di sapere<br><br><br>quali nuove condizioni la vita ci imporrà.<br>Proprio quando crediamo di aver saldato il conto<br>la vita ce ne presenta uno nuovo, più difficile da pagare.<br>— Ora vado a sedermi un poco con le mie porcellane. |  
| COLBY | Mi scusi, ma devo ricordarle:<br><br><br>ha quella riunione nella [[motifs/city\|City]]<br>domani mattina. Mi aveva chiesto di preparare<br>certe cifre per lei. Le ho qui. |  
| SIR CLAUDE | Molto dipende da mia moglie. Sia paziente con lei, Colby.<br><br><br>— Oh sì, quella riunione. Dobbiamo passare in rassegna le cifre.<br>SIPARIO |



<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_2--scene_1" data-title="The Confidential Clerk — Act II" data-chapter="Scene 1" data-kind="scene" data-tags="archetype/child,motif/childhood,motif/city,motif/clock,motif/cross,motif/desert,motif/flowers,motif/garden,motif/ghosts,motif/love,motif/marriage,motif/night,motif/spring,motif/time,motif/well,concept/courage,concept/doubt,concept/education,concept/eternity,concept/grotesque,concept/hope,concept/loneliness,concept/providence,concept/self,concept/solitude,concept/suffering,concept/truth,concept/wonder,setting/desert,setting/london"></span>


| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | The flat in the mews a few weeks later. COLBY is seated at the piano; LUCASTA in an armchair. The concluding bars of a piece of music are heard as the curtain rises. |
| LUCASTA | I think you play awfully [[motifs/well\|well]], Colby —<br><br><br>Not that my opinion counts for anything:<br><br><br>You know that. But I’d like to learn about music.<br>I wish you would teach me how to appreciate it. |
| COLBY | I don’t think that you’ll need much teaching;<br><br><br>Not at this stage, anyway. All you need at first<br>Is to hear more music. And to find out what you like.<br>When you know what you like, and begin to know it [[motifs/well\|well]],<br>Then you will want to learn about its structure<br>And the various forms, and the different ways of playing it. |
| LUCASTA | But suppose I only like the wrong things? |
| COLBY | No, I’m sure you’ll prefer the right things, when you hear them.<br><br><br>I’ve given you a test. Several of the pieces<br>That I’ve just played you were very second-rate,<br>And you didn’t like them. You liked the right ones. |
| LUCASTA | Colby, I didn’t know you were so artful!<br><br><br>So the things I liked were the right ones to like?<br>Still, I’m awfully ignorant. Can you believe<br>That I’ve never been to a concert in my life?<br>I only go to shows when somebody invites me,<br>And no one has ever asked me to a concert.<br>I’ve been to the Opera, of course, several times,<br>But I’m afraid I never really listened to the music:<br>I just enjoyed going — to see the other people,<br>And to be seen there! And because you feel out of it<br>If you never go to the Opera, in the season.<br>Though I’ve always felt out of it. And can you realise<br>That nobody has ever played to me before? |
| COLBY | And this is the first [[motifs/time\|time]] I’ve played to anyone … |
| LUCASTA | Don’t be such a fraud. You know you told me<br><br><br>The piano was only delivered this week<br>And you had it tuned yesterday. Still, I’m flattered<br>To be your first visitor in this flat<br>And to be the first to hear you play this piano. |
| COLBY | That’s not what I meant. I mean that I’ve not played<br><br><br>To anyone, since I came to the conclusion<br>That I should never become a musician. |
| LUCASTA | Did you find it a strain, then, playing to me? |
| COLBY | As a matter of fact, I think I played better.<br><br><br>I can’t bring myself to play to other people,<br>And when I’m [[Alone\|alone]] I can’t forget<br>That it’s only myself to whom I’m playing.<br>But with you, it was neither [[concepts/solitude\|solitude]] nor … people. |
| LUCASTA | I’m glad I’m not people. Will you play to me again<br><br><br>And teach me about music? |
| COLBY | Yes, of course I will.<br><br><br>But I’m sure that when you learn about music —<br>And that won’t take you long — and hear good performers,<br>You’ll very quickly realise how bad my playing is. |
| LUCASTA | Really, Colby, you do make difficulties!<br><br><br>But what about taking me to a concert? |
| COLBY | Only the other day, I invited you … |
| LUCASTA | To go to see that American Musical! |
| COLBY | [[motifs/well\|Well]], I’d heard you say you wanted to see it. |
| LUCASTA | But not with you! |
| COLBY | You made that very clear.<br><br><br>But why not with me? |
| LUCASTA | Because you don’t like them —<br><br><br>American Musicals. Do you think it’s any compliment<br>To invite a woman to something she would like<br>When she knows you wouldn’t like it? That’s not a compliment:<br><br><br>That’s just being … patronising. But if you invite me<br>To something you like — that is a compliment.<br><br><br>It shows you want to educate me. |
| COLBY | But I didn’t know<br><br><br>That you wanted to be educated. |
| LUCASTA | Neither did I.<br><br><br>But I wanted you to want to educate me;<br>And now I’m beginning to believe that I want it. |
| COLBY | [[motifs/well\|Well]], I’m going to invite you to the next concert … |
| LUCASTA | The next that you want to go to yourself. |
| COLBY | And perhaps you’ll let me tell you beforehand<br><br><br>About the programme — or the things I want to hear.<br>I’ll play you the themes, so you’ll recognise them.<br>Better still, I’ll play you the gramophone records. |
| LUCASTA | I’d rather you played me bits yourself, and explained them.<br><br><br>We’ll begin my [[concepts/education\|education]] at once. |
| COLBY | I suspect that it’s you who are educating me. |
| LUCASTA | Colby, you really are full of surprises!<br><br><br>I’ve never met a man so ignorant as you<br>Yet knowing so much that one wouldn’t suspect.<br>Perhaps that’s why I like you. |
| COLBY | That’s not quite the reason. |
| LUCASTA | Oh, so you believe that I like you?<br><br><br>I didn’t know that you were so conceited. |
| COLBY | No, it’s not conceit — the reason that I’m thinking of.<br><br><br>It’s something quite simple. |
| LUCASTA | Then I wish you’d tell me.<br><br><br>Because I don’t know. |
| COLBY | The first [[motifs/time\|time]] we met<br><br><br>You were trying very hard to give a false impression.<br>And then you came to see that you hadn’t succeeded. |
| LUCASTA | Oh, so I was trying to give a false impression?<br><br><br>What sort of impression was I trying to give? |
| COLBY | That doesn’t really matter. But, for some reason,<br><br><br>You thought I’d get a false impression anyway.<br>You preferred it to be one of your own creation<br>Rather than wait to see what happened.<br>I [[concepts/hope\|hope]] you don’t mind: I know it sounds impertinent. |
| LUCASTA | [[motifs/well\|Well]], there’s one thing you haven’t learnt yet,<br><br><br>And that is, to know when you’re paying a compliment.<br>That was a compliment. And a very clever one. |
| COLBY | I admit that at first I was very bewildered<br><br><br>By you … and B. |
| LUCASTA | Oh, by me … and B. |
| COLBY | Only afterwards,<br><br><br>When I had seen you a number of times,<br>I decided that was only your kind of [[concepts/self\|self]]-defence. |
| LUCASTA | What made you think it was [[concepts/self\|self]]-defence? |
| COLBY | Because you couldn’t wait to see what happened.<br><br><br>You’re afraid of what would happen if you left things to themselves.<br>You jump — because you’re afraid of being pushed.<br>I think that you’re brave — and I think that you’re frightened.<br>Perhaps you’ve been very badly hurt, at some [[motifs/time\|time]].<br>Or at least, there may have been something in your life<br>To rob you of any sense of security. |
| LUCASTA | And I’m sure you have that — the sense of security. |
| COLBY | No, I haven’t either. |
| LUCASTA | There, I don’t believe you.<br><br><br>What did I think till now? Oh, it’s strange, isn’t it,<br>That as one gets to know a person better<br>One rinds them in some ways very like oneself,<br>In unexpected ways. And then you begin<br>To discover differences inside the likeness.<br>You may feel insecure, in some ways —<br><br><br>But your insecurity is nothing like mine. |
| COLBY | In what way is it different? |
| LUCASTA | It’s hard to explain.<br><br><br>Perhaps it’s something that your music stands for.<br>There’s one thing I know. When you first told me<br>What a disaster it was in your life<br>When you found that you’d never be a good musician —<br>Of course, I don’t know whether you were right.<br><br><br>For all I can tell, you may have been mistaken,<br>And perhaps you could be a very great musician:<br>But that’s not the point. You’d convinced yourself;<br>And you felt that your life had all collapsed<br>And that you must learn to do something different.<br>And so you applied for Eggerson’s position,<br>And made up your mind to go into business<br>And be someone like Claude … or B. I was sorry,<br>Very sorry for you. I admired your [[concepts/courage\|courage]]<br>In facing facts — or the facts as you saw them.<br>And yet, all the [[motifs/time\|time]], I found I envied you<br><br><br>And I didn’t know why! And now I think I know.<br>It’s awful for a man to have to give up,<br>A career that he’s set his heart on, I’m sure:<br>But it’s only the outer world that you’ve lost:<br>You’ve still got your inner world — a world that’s more real.<br>That’s why you’re different from the rest of us:<br>You have your secret [[motifs/garden\|garden]]; to which you can retire<br>And lock the gate behind you. |
| COLBY | And lock the gate behind me?<br><br><br>Are you sure that you haven’t your own secret [[motifs/garden\|garden]]<br>Somewhere, if you could find it? |
| LUCASTA | If I could find it!<br><br><br>No, my only [[motifs/garden\|garden]] is … a dirty public square<br>In a shabby part of [[settings/london\|London]] — like the one where I lived<br>For a [[motifs/time\|time]], with my mother. I’ve no [[motifs/garden\|garden]].<br>I hardly feel that I’m even a person:<br>Nothing but a bit of living matter<br>Floating on the surface of the Regent’s Canal.<br>Floating, that’s it. |
| COLBY | You’re very much a person.<br><br><br>I’m sure that there is a [[motifs/garden\|garden]] somewhere for you —<br>For anyone who wants one as much as you do. |
| LUCASTA | And your [[motifs/garden\|garden]] is a [[motifs/garden\|garden]]<br><br><br>Where you hear a music that no one else could hear,<br>And the [[motifs/flowers\|flowers]] have a scent that no one else could smell. |
| COLBY | You may be right, up to a point.<br><br><br>And yet, you know, it’s not quite real to me —<br>Although it’s as real to me as … this world.<br>But that’s just the trouble. They seem so unrelated.<br>I turn the key, and walk through the gate,<br>And there I am … [[Alone\|alone]], in my ‘[[motifs/garden\|garden]]’.<br>[[works/alone-(poe)\|Alone]], that’s the thing. That’s why it’s not real.<br>You know, I think that Eggerson’s [[motifs/garden\|garden]]<br>Is more real than mine. |
| LUCASTA | Eggerson’s [[motifs/garden\|garden]]?<br><br><br>What makes you think of Eggerson — of all people? |
| COLBY | [[motifs/well\|Well]], he retires to his [[motifs/garden\|garden]] — literally,<br><br><br>And also in the same sense that I retire to mine.<br>But he doesn’t feel [[Alone\|alone]] there. And when he comes out<br>He has marrows, or beetroot, or peas … for Mrs. Eggerson. |
| LUCASTA | Are you laughing at me? |
| COLBY | I’m being very serious.<br><br><br>What I mean is, my [[motifs/garden\|garden]]’s no less unreal to me<br>Than the world outside it. If you have two lives<br>Which have nothing whatever to do with each other —<br>[[motifs/well\|Well]], they’re both unreal. But for Eggerson<br>His [[motifs/garden\|garden]] is a part of one single world. |
| LUCASTA | But what do you want? |
| COLBY | Not to be [[Alone\|alone]] there.<br><br><br>If I were religious, God would walk in my [[motifs/garden\|garden]]<br>And that would make the world outside it real<br>And acceptable, I think. |
| LUCASTA | You sound awfully religious.<br><br><br>Is there no other way of making it real to you? |
| COLBY | It’s simply the fact of being [[Alone\|alone]] there<br><br><br>That makes it unreal. |
| LUCASTA | Can no one else enter? |
| COLBY | It can’t be done by issuing invitations:<br><br><br>They would just have to come. And I should not see them coming.<br>I should not hear the opening of the gate.<br>They would simply … be there suddenly,<br>Unexpectedly. Walking down an alley<br>I should become aware of someone walking with me.<br>That’s the only way I can think of putting it. |
| LUCASTA | How afraid one is of … being hurt! |
| COLBY | It’s not the hurting that one would mind<br><br><br>But the sense of desolation afterwards. |
| LUCASTA | I know what you mean. Then the [[motifs/flowers\|flowers]] would fade<br><br><br>And the music would stop. And the walls would be broken.<br>And you would find yourself in a devastated area —<br>A bomb-site … willow-herb … a dirty public square.<br>But I can’t imagine that happening to you.<br>You seem so secure, to me. Not only in your music —<br>That’s just its expression. You don’t seem to me<br>To need anybody. |
| COLBY | That’s quite untrue. |
| LUCASTA | But you’ve something else, that I haven’t got:<br><br><br>Something of which the music is a … symbol.<br>I really would like to understand music,<br>Not in order to be able to talk about it,<br>But … partly, to enjoy it … and because of what it stands for.<br>You know, I’m a little jealous of your music!<br>When I see it as a means of contact with a world<br>More real than any I’ve ever lived in.<br><br><br>And I’d like to understand you. |
| COLBY | I believe you do already,<br><br><br>Better than … other people. And I want to understand you.<br>Does one ever come to understand anyone? |
| LUCASTA | I think you’re being very discouraging:<br><br><br>Are you doing it deliberately? |
| COLBY | That’s not what I meant.<br><br><br>I meant, there’s no end to understanding a person.<br>All one can do is to understand them better,<br>To keep up with them; so that as the other changes<br>You can understand the change as soon as it happens,<br>Though you couldn’t have predicted it. |
| LUCASTA | I think I’m changing.<br><br><br>I’ve changed quite a lot in the last two hours. |
| COLBY | And I think I’m changing too. But perhaps what we call change … |
| LUCASTA | Is understanding better what one really is.<br><br><br>And the reason why that comes about, perhaps … |
| COLBY | Is, beginning to understand another person. |
| LUCASTA | Oh Colby, now that we begin to understand,<br><br><br>I’d like you to know a little more about me.<br>You must have wondered. |
| COLBY | Must have wondered?<br><br><br>No, I haven’t wondered. It’s all a strange world<br>To me, you know, in which I find myself.<br>But if you mean, wondered about your … background:<br>No. I’ve been curious to know what you are,<br>But not who you are, in the ordinary sense.<br>Is that what you mean? I’ve just accepted you. |
| LUCASTA | Oh, that’s so wonderful, to be accepted!<br><br><br>No one has ever ‘just accepted’ me before.<br>Of course the facts don’t matter, in a sense.<br>But now we’ve got to this point — you might as [[motifs/well\|well]] know them. |
| COLBY | I’d gladly tell you everything about myself;<br><br><br>But you know most of what there is to say<br>Already, either from what I’ve told you<br>Or from what I’ve told B.; or from Sir Claude. |
| LUCASTA | Claude hasn’t told me anything about you;<br><br><br>He doesn’t tell me much. And as for B. —<br>I’d much rather hear it from yourself. |
| COLBY | There’s only one thing I can’t tell you.<br><br><br>At least, not yet. I’m not allowed to tell.<br>And that’s about my parents. |
| LUCASTA | Oh, I see.<br><br><br>[[motifs/well\|Well]], I can’t believe that matters.<br>But I can tell you all about my parents:<br><br><br>At least, I’m going to. |
| COLBY | Does that matter, either? |
| LUCASTA | In one way, it matters. A little while ago<br><br><br>You said, very cleverly, that when we first met<br>You saw I was trying to give a false impression.<br>I want to tell you now, why I tried to do that.<br>And it’s always succeeded with people before:<br>I got into the habit of giving that impression.<br>That’s where B. has been such a help to me —<br>He fosters the impression. He half believes in it.<br>But he knows all about me, and he knows<br>That what some men have thought about me wasn’t true. |
| COLBY | What wasn’t true? |
| LUCASTA | That I was Claude’s mistress —<br><br><br>Or had been his mistress, palmed off on B. |
| COLBY | I never thought of such a thing! |
| LUCASTA | You never thought of such a thing!<br><br><br>There are not many men who wouldn’t have thought it.<br>I don’t know about B. He’s very generous.<br>I don’t think he’d have minded. But he’s very clever too;<br>And he guessed the [[concepts/truth\|truth]] from the very first moment. |
| COLBY | But what is there to know? |
| LUCASTA | You’ll laugh when I tell you:<br><br><br>I’m only Claude’s daughter. |
| COLBY | His daughter! |
| LUCASTA | His daughter. Oh, it’s a sordid story.<br><br><br>I hated my mother. I never could see<br>How Claude had ever liked her. Oh, that [[motifs/childhood\|childhood]] —<br>Always living in seedy lodgings<br>And being turned out when the neighbours complained.<br>Oh of course Claude gave her money, a regular allowance;<br>But it wouldn’t have mattered how much he’d given her:<br>It was always spent before the end of the quarter<br>On gin and betting, I should guess.<br>And I knew how she supplemented her income<br>When I was sent out. I’ve been locked in a cupboard!<br>I was only eight years old<br>When she died of an ‘accidental overdose’.<br>Then Claude took me over. That was lucky.<br>But I was old enough to remember … too much. |
| COLBY | You are Claude’s daughter! |
| LUCASTA | Oh, there’s no [[concepts/doubt\|doubt]] of that.<br><br><br>I’m sure he wished there had been. He’s been good to me<br>In his way. But I’m always a reminder to him<br>Of something he would prefer to forget. |
| *(didascalia)* | [A pause] |
| LUCASTA | But why don’t you say something? Are you shocked? |
| COLBY | Shocked? No. Yes. You don’t understand.<br><br><br>I want to explain. But I can’t, just yet.<br>Oh, why did I ever come into this [[house]]!<br>Lucasta … |
| LUCASTA | I can see [[motifs/well\|well]] enough you are shocked.<br><br><br>You ought to see your face! I’m disappointed.<br>I suppose that’s all. I believe you’re more shocked<br>Than if I’d told you I was Claude’s mistress.<br><br><br>Claude has always been ashamed of me:<br>Now you’re ashamed of me. I thought you’d understand.<br><br><br>Little you know what it’s like to be a bastard<br>And wanted by nobody. I know why you’re shocked:<br>Claude has just accepted me like a debit item<br>Always in his cash account. I don’t like myself.<br>I don’t like the person I’ve forced myself to be;<br>And I liked you because you didn’t like that person either,<br>And I thought you’d come to see me as the real kind of person<br>That I want to be. That I know I am.<br>That was new to me. I suppose I was flattered.<br>And I thought, now, perhaps, if someone else sees me<br>As I really am, I might become myself. |
| COLBY | Oh Lucasta, I’m not shocked. Not by you,<br><br><br>Not by anything you think. It’s to do with myself. |
| LUCASTA | Yourself, indeed! Your precious [[concepts/self\|self]]!<br><br><br>Why don’t you shut yourself up in that [[motifs/garden\|garden]]<br>Where you like to be [[Alone\|alone]] with yourself?<br>Or perhaps you think it would be bad for your prospects<br>Now that you’re Claude’s white-headed boy.<br>Perhaps he’ll adopt you, and make you his heir<br>And you’ll marry another Lady Elizabeth.<br>But in that event, Colby, you’ll have to accept me<br>As your sister! Even if I am a guttersnipe … |
| COLBY | You mustn’t use such words! You don’t know how it’s hurting. |
| LUCASTA | I could use words much stronger than that,<br><br><br>And I will, if I choose. Oh, I’m sorry:<br>I suppose it’s my mother coming out in me.<br>You know, Colby, I’m truly disappointed.<br>I was sure, when I told you all I did,<br>That you wouldn’t mind at all. That you might be sorry for me.<br>But now I don’t want you to be sorry, thank you.<br>Why, I’d actually thought of telling you before,<br>And I postponed telling you, just for the fun of it:<br>I thought, when I tell him, it will be so wonderful<br>All in a moment. And now there’s nothing,<br>Nothing at all. It’s far worse than ever.<br>Just when you think you’re on the point of release<br>From [[concepts/loneliness\|loneliness]], then [[concepts/loneliness\|loneliness]] swoops down upon you;<br>When you think you’re getting out, you’re getting further in,<br>And you know at last that there’s no escape.<br>[[motifs/well\|Well]], I’ll be going. |
| COLBY | You mustn’t go yet!<br><br><br>There’s something else that I want to explain,<br>And now I’m going to. I’m breaking a promise. But … |
| LUCASTA | I don’t believe there’s anything to explain<br><br><br>That could explain anything away. I shall never<br>Never forget that look on your face<br>When I told you about Claude and my mother.<br>I may be a bastard, but I have some [[concepts/self\|self]]-respect.<br>[[motifs/well\|Well]], there’s always B. I think that now<br>I’m just beginning to appreciate B. |
| COLBY | Lucasta, wait! |
| *(didascalia)* | [Enter B. KAGHAN] |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter B. Kaghan. |
| KAGHAN | To see the new flat. And here’s Lucasta.<br>I knew I should find she’d got in first!<br>Trust Kaghan’s intuitions! I’m your guardian angel,<br>Colby, to protect you from Lucasta. |
| LUCASTA | You’re my guardian angel at the moment, B.<br><br><br>You’re to take me out to dinner. And I’m dying for a drink. |
| KAGHAN | I told Colby, never learn to mix cocktails,<br><br><br>If you don’t want women always dropping in on you.<br>And between a couple of man-eating tigers<br>Like you and Lizzie, he’s got to have protection. |
| LUCASTA | Colby doesn’t need your protection racket<br><br><br>So far as I’m concerned, B. And as for Lizzie,<br>You’d better not get in her way when she’s hunting.<br><br><br>But all that matters now is, that I’m hungry,<br>And you’ve got to give me a very good dinner. |
| KAGHAN | You shall be fed. All in good [[motifs/time\|time]].<br><br><br>I’ve come to inspect the new bachelor quarters,<br>And to wish Colby luck. I’ve always been lucky,<br>And I always bring luck to other people. |
| COLBY | Will you have a glass of sherry? |
| KAGHAN | Yes, I’ll have a glass of sherry,<br><br><br>To drink [[success]] to the flat. Lucasta too:<br>Much better for you than cocktails, Lucasta. |
| LUCASTA | You know I don’t like sherry. |
| KAGHAN | You’ve got to drink it,<br><br><br>To Colby, and a happy bachelor life!<br>Which depends, of course, on preventing Lizzie<br>From always interfering. Be firm with her, Colby;<br>Assert your right to a little privacy.<br>Now’s the moment for firmness. Don’t let her [[motifs/cross\|cross]] the threshold. |
| LUCASTA | As if you weren’t as afraid of her as anybody! |
| KAGHAN | [[motifs/well\|Well]], at least, I’ve always managed to escape her. |
| LUCASTA | Only because she’s never wanted to pursue you. |
| KAGHAN | Yes, I made a bad impression at the start:<br><br><br>I saw that it was necessary. I’m afraid Colby<br>Has made a good impression; which he’ll have to live down.<br>— I must say, I like the way you’ve bad the place done up. |
| COLBY | It was Lady Elizabeth chose the decorations. |
| KAGHAN | Then I’m not sure I like them. You must change the colours.<br><br><br>It’s all a bit too dim. You need something brighter.<br>But otherwise, it looks pretty comfortable.<br>If I was as snug as Colby is, Lucasta,<br>I’d never have thought of changing my condition. |
| LUCASTA | You’re always free to think again. |
| KAGHAN | [[motifs/marriage\|Marriage]] is a gamble. But I’m a born gambler<br><br><br>And I’ve put my shirt … no, not quite the right expression —<br>Lucasta’s the most exciting speculation<br>I’ve ever thought of investing in.<br>Colby’s more cautious. You know, Colby,<br>You and I ought to be in business together.<br>I’m a good guesser. But I sometimes guess wrong.<br>I make decisions on the spur of the moment,<br>But you’d never take a leap in the dark;<br>You’d keep me on the rails. |
| COLBY | That’s just nonsense.<br><br><br>You only pretend that you’re a gambler.<br>You’ve got as level a head as anyone,<br>And you never get involved in anything risky.<br>You like to pretend to other people<br>That you’re a gambler. I don’t believe you ever gamble<br>On anything that isn’t a certainty. |
| KAGHAN | [[motifs/well\|Well]], there’s something in that. You know, Lucasta,<br><br><br>Colby is a good judge of character. |
| LUCASTA | You’d need to be a better judge of character<br><br><br>Yourself, before you said that of Colby. |
| KAGHAN | Oh, I’m a good judge. Now, I’ll tell you the difference<br><br><br>Between ourselves and Colby. You and me —<br>The one thing we want is security<br><br><br>And respectability! Now Colby<br>Doesn’t really care about being respectable —<br>He was born and bred to it. I wasn’t, Colby.<br>Do you know, I was a foundling? You didn’t know that!<br>Never had any parents. Just adopted, from nowhere.<br>That’s why I want to be a power in the [[motifs/city\|City]],<br>On the boards of all the solidest companies:<br>Because I’ve no background — no background at all.<br>That’s one thing I like about Lucasta:<br>She doesn’t despise me. |
| LUCASTA | Nobody could despise you.<br><br><br>And what’s more important, you don’t despise me. |
| KAGHAN | Nobody could despise you, Lucasta;<br><br><br>And we want the same things. But as for Colby,<br>He’s the sort of fellow who might chuck it all<br>And go to live on a [[motifs/desert\|desert]] island.<br>But I [[concepts/hope\|hope]] you won’t do that. We need you where you are. |
| COLBY | I’m beginning to believe you’ve a pretty shrewd insight<br><br><br>Into things that have nothing to do with business. |
| KAGHAN | And you have a very sound head for business.<br><br><br>Maybe you’re a better financier than I am!<br>That’s why we ought to be in business together. |
| LUCASTA | You’re both very good at paying compliments;<br><br><br>But I remarked that I was hungry. |
| KAGHAN | You can’t want dinner yet.<br><br><br>It’s only six o’[[motifs/clock\|clock]]. We can’t dine till eight;<br>Not at any restaurant that you like.<br><br><br>— For a change, let’s talk about Lucasta.<br>LUCASTA [rising]. If you want to discuss me … |
| *(didascalia)* | [A knock at the door. Enter LADY ELIZABETH] |
| LADY ELIZABETH | Oh, good evening.<br><br><br>Good evening, Mr. Kaghan. Good evening, Lucasta.<br>Have you just arrived, or are you just leaving? |
| LUCASTA | We’re on the point of leaving, Lady Elizabeth. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | I’ve come over to have a look at the flat<br><br><br>Now that you’ve moved in. Because you can’t tell<br>Whether a scheme of decoration<br>Is right, until the place has been lived in<br><br><br>By the person for whom it was designed.<br>So I have to see you in it. Did you say you were leaving? |
| KAGHAN | We’re going out to dinner. Lucasta’s very hungry. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | Hungry? At six o’[[motifs/clock\|clock]]? Where will you get dinner?<br><br><br>Oh, I know. It’s a chance to try that Herbal Restaurant<br>I recommended to you. You can have dinner early:<br>Most of its patrons dine at half past six.<br>They have the most delicious salads!<br>And I told you, Mr. Kaghan, you’re the type of person<br>Who needs to eat a great deal of salad.<br>You remember, I made you take a note of the address;<br>And I don’t believe that you’ve been there yet. |
| KAGHAN | Why no, as a matter of fact, I haven’t.<br><br><br>I’ve kept meaning to. Shall we go there, Lucasta? |
| LUCASTA | I’m so hungry, I could even eat a herbal salad. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | That’s right. Just mention my name, Mr. Kaghan,<br><br><br>And ask for the table in the left hand corner:<br>It has the best waitress. Good [[motifs/night\|night]]. |
| LUCASTA | Good [[motifs/night\|night]]. |
| KAGHAN | And thank you so much. You give such good advice. |
| *(didascalia)* | [Exeunt KAGHAN and LUCASTA] |
| LADY ELIZABETH | Were those young people here by appointment?<br><br><br>Or did they come in unexpectedly? |
| COLBY | I’d invited Lucasta. She had asked me to play to her. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | You call her Lucasta? Young people nowadays<br><br><br>Seem to have dropped the use of surnames altogether.<br>But, Colby, I [[concepts/hope\|hope]] you won’t mind a gentle hint.<br>I feared it was possible you might become too friendly<br>With Mr. Kaghan and Miss Angel.<br>I can see you’ve lived a rather sheltered life,<br>And I’ve noticed them paying you a good deal of attention.<br>You see, you’re rather a curiosity<br>To both of them — you’re not the sort of person<br>They ever meet in their kind of society.<br>So naturally, they want to take you up.<br>I can speak more freely, as an elderly person. |
| COLBY | But, Lady Elizabeth … |
| LADY ELIZABETH | [[motifs/well\|Well]], older than you are,<br><br><br>And a good deal wiser in the ways of the world. |
| COLBY | But, Lady Elizabeth, what is it you object to?<br><br><br>They’re both intelligent … and kind. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | Oh, I don’t say they’re not intelligent and kind.<br><br><br>I’m not making any malicious suggestions:<br>But they are rather worldly and materialistic,<br>And … [[motifs/well\|well]], rather vulgar. They’re not your sort at all. |
| COLBY | I shouldn’t call them vulgar. Perhaps I’m vulgar too.<br><br><br>But what, do you think, is my sort?<br><br><br>I don’t know, myself. And I should like to know. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | In the first place, you ought to mix with people of breeding.<br><br><br>I said to myself, when I first saw you,<br>‘He is very [[motifs/well\|well]] bred’. I knew nothing about you,<br>But one doesn’t need to know, if one knows what breeding is.<br>And, second, you need intellectual society.<br>Now, that already limits your acquaintance:<br>Because, what’s surprising, [[motifs/well\|well]]-bred people<br>Are sometimes far from intellectual;<br>And — what’s less surprising — intellectual people<br>Are often ill-bred. But that’s not all.<br>You need intellectual, [[motifs/well\|well]]-bred people<br>Of spirituality — and that’s the rarest. |
| COLBY | That would limit my acquaintance to a very small number,<br><br><br>And I don’t know where to find them. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | They can be found.<br><br><br>But I came to have a look at the flat<br>To see if the colour scheme really suited you.<br>I believe it does. The walls; and the curtains;<br>And most of the furniture. But, that writing-table!<br>Where did that writing-table come from? |
| COLBY | It’s an office desk. Sir Claude got it for me.<br><br><br>I said I needed a desk in my room:<br>You see, I shall do a good deal of my work here. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | And what is that shrouded object on it?<br><br><br>Don’t tell me it’s a typewriter. |
| COLBY | It is a typewriter.<br><br><br>I’ve already begun to work here. At the moment<br>I’m working on a company report. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | I hadn’t reckoned on reports and typewriters<br><br><br>When I designed this room. |
| COLBY | It’s the sort of room I wanted.<br><br><br>LADY ELIZABETH [rising]. And I see a photograph in a silver frame.<br><br><br>I’m afraid I shall have to instruct you, Colby.<br>Photographic portraits — even in silver frames —<br>Are much too intimate for the sitting-room.<br>May I remove it? Surely your bedroom<br>Is the proper place for photographic souvenirs. |
| *(didascalia)* | [She sits down, holding the portrait] |
| COLBY | What was I going to say? Oh, I know.<br>Do you believe in reincarnation? |
| COLBY | No, I don’t. I mean, I’ve never thought about it. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | I can’t say that I believe in it.<br><br><br>I did, for a [[motifs/time\|time]]. I studied the doctrine.<br>But I was going to say, if I believed in it<br><br><br>I should have said that we had known each other<br>In some previous incarnation. — Is this your mother? |
| COLBY | No, that is my aunt. I never knew my mother.<br><br><br>She died when I was born. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | She died when you were born.<br><br><br>Have you other near relatives? Brothers or sisters? |
| COLBY | No brothers or sisters. No. As for other relatives,<br><br><br>I never knew any, when I was a [[archetypes/child\|child]].<br>I suppose I’ve never been interested … in relatives. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | You did not want to know your relatives!<br><br><br>I understand exactly how you felt.<br>How I disliked my parents! I had a governess;<br>Several, in fact. And I loathed them all.<br>Were you brought up by a governess? |
| COLBY | No. By my aunt. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | And did you loathe her? No, of course not.<br><br><br>Or you wouldn’t have her portrait. If you never knew your parents …<br>But was your father living? |
| COLBY | I never knew my father. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | Then, if you never had a governess,<br><br><br>And if you never knew either of your parents,<br>You can’t understand what loathing really is.<br>Yet we must have some similarity of background. |
| COLBY | But you had parents. And no [[concepts/doubt\|doubt]], many relatives. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | Oh, swarms of relatives! And such unpleasant people!<br><br><br>I thought of myself as a dove in an eagle’s nest.<br>They were so carnivorous. Always killing things and eating them.<br>And yet our [[motifs/childhood\|childhood]] must have been similar.<br>These are only superficial differences:<br>You must have been a lonely [[archetypes/child\|child]], having no relatives —<br>No brothers or sisters — and I was lonely<br>Because they were so numerous — and so uncongenial.<br>They made me feel an outcast. And yet they were so commonplace.<br>Do you know, Colby, when I was a [[archetypes/child\|child]]<br>I had three obsessions, and I never told anyone.<br>I [[concepts/wonder\|wonder]] if you had the same obsessions? |
| COLBY | What were they? |
| LADY ELIZABETH | The first was, that I was very ugly<br><br><br>And didn’t know it. Then, that I was feeble-minded<br>And didn’t know it. Finally,<br>That I was a foundling, and didn’t know it.<br>Of course, I was terrified of being ugly,<br>And of being feeble-minded: though my family made me think so.<br>But you know, I actually liked to believe<br><br><br>That I was a foundling — or do I mean ‘changeling’? |
| COLBY | I don’t know which you mean. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | However that may be,<br><br><br>I didn’t want to belong there. I refused to believe<br>That my father could have been an ordinary earl!<br>And I couldn’t believe that my mother was my mother.<br><br><br>These were foolish fancies. I was a silly girl,<br>And very romantic. But it goes to show<br>How different I felt myself to be<br>And then I took up the Wisdom of the East<br>And believed, for a while, in reincarnation.<br>That seemed to explain it all. I don’t believe it now.<br>That was only a phase. But it made it all so simple!<br>To be able to think that one’s earthly parents<br>Are only the means that we have to employ<br>To become reincarnate. And that one’s real ancestry<br>Is one’s previous existences. Of course, there’s something in us,<br>In all of us, which isn’t just heredity,<br>But something unique. Something we have been<br>From [[concepts/eternity\|eternity]]. Something … straight from God.<br>That means that we are nearer to God than to anyone.<br>— Where did you live, as a [[archetypes/child\|child]]? |
| COLBY | In Teddington. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | Teddington? In what county? |
| COLBY | It’s very close to [[settings/london\|London]]. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | Still, you were brought up, like me, in the country.<br><br><br>Teddington. I seem to have heard of it.<br>Was it a large [[house]]? |
| COLBY | No, a very small one. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | But you had your aunt. And she was devoted to you,<br><br><br>I have no [[concepts/doubt\|doubt]]. What is your aunt’s name?<br>Is it Simpkins? |
| COLBY | No, a married aunt.<br><br><br>A widow. Her name is Mrs. Guzzard. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | Guzzard? Did you say Guzzard? An unusual name.<br><br><br>Guzzard, did you say? The name means something to me.<br>Yes. Guzzard. That is the name I’ve been hunting for! |
| COLBY | You may have come across the name before;<br><br><br>Although, as you say, it is an uncommon one.<br>You couldn’t have known my aunt. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | No. I never met … your aunt.<br><br><br>But the name is familiar. How old are you, Colby? |
| COLBY | I’m twenty-five. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | Twenty-five. What became of your father? |
| COLBY | [[motifs/well\|Well]] … I didn’t have a father.<br><br><br>You see … I was an illegitimate [[archetypes/child\|child]]. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | Oh yes. An illegitimate [[archetypes/child\|child]].<br><br><br>So that the only relative you knew<br>Was Mrs. Guzzard. And you always called her ‘aunt’? |
| COLBY | Why not? She was my aunt. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | And as for your mother —<br><br><br>Mrs. Guzzard’s sister, I suppose … |
| COLBY | Her sister — which makes Mrs. Guzzard my aunt. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | And are you quite sure that Mrs. Guzzard’s sister —<br><br><br>Who you say was your mother — really was your mother? |
| COLBY | Why, Lady Elizabeth! Why should I [[concepts/doubt\|doubt]] it?<br><br><br>That is not the kind of story my aunt would invent. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | Not if she is your aunt. Did Mrs. Guzzard<br><br><br>And Mr. Guzzard — have any children? |
| COLBY | They had no children of their own.<br><br><br>That is to say, they had had one little boy<br>Who died when I was very young indeed.<br>I don’t remember him. I was told about him.<br>But I can’t help wondering why you are so interested:<br>There’s nothing very interesting about my background —<br>I assure you there isn’t. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | It may be more interesting<br><br><br>Than you are aware of. Colby … |
| *(didascalia)* | [A knock on the door] |
| LADY ELIZABETH |                                                          Who’s that? |
| *(didascalia)* | [Enter SIR CLAUDE] |
| SIR CLAUDE | Elizabeth! I was told that you were here with Colby.<br><br><br>So I came over instead of telephoning,<br>Just to give him these notes. They’re notes for my speech<br>At the dinner of the Potters’ Company. |
| COLBY | That’s tomorrow [[motifs/night\|night]], I believe. |
| SIR CLAUDE | Yes it is.<br>But you know that I’ll have to have my speech written out<br>And then memorise it. I can’t use notes:<br>It’s got to sound spontaneous. I’ve jotted down some headings.<br>Just see if you can develop them for me<br>With a few striking phrases. It should last about ten minutes.<br>And then we’ll go over it tomorrow.<br>COLBY [looking at the notes].                 I’ll try. |
| SIR CLAUDE | It’s just in ways like this, Elizabeth,<br><br><br>That Colby can be of greater help than Eggerson.<br>I couldn’t have asked Eggerson to write a speech for me.<br>Oh, by the way, Colby, how’s the piano? |
| COLBY | It’s a wonderful piano. I’ve never played<br><br><br>On such an instrument. It’s much too good for me. |
| SIR CLAUDE | You need a good piano. You’ll play all the better. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | Claude! |
| SIR CLAUDE | What is it, Elizabeth? |
| LADY ELIZABETH | I’ve just made a startling discovery!<br><br><br>All through a name — and intuition.<br>But it shall be proved. The [[concepts/truth\|truth]] has come out.<br>It’s Colby. Colby is my lost [[archetypes/child\|child]]! |
| SIR CLAUDE | What? Your [[archetypes/child\|child]], Elizabeth? What on earth makes you think so? |
| LADY ELIZABETH | I must see this Mrs. Guzzard. I must confront her.<br><br><br>This couldn’t possibly be a coincidence.<br>It seems incredible, doesn’t it, Claude?<br>And yet it would be still more incredible<br>If it were only a coincidence.<br>Perhaps I ought not to believe it yet,<br>Perhaps it is wrong of me to feel so sure,<br>But it seems that [[concepts/providence\|Providence]] has brought you back to me,<br>And you, Claude, and Eggerson have been the instruments.<br>I must be right. Claude, tell me I am right. |
| SIR CLAUDE | But Elizabeth, what has led you to believe<br><br><br>That Colby is your son? |
| LADY ELIZABETH | Oh, I forgot<br><br><br>In my excitement: you arrived the very moment<br>When the [[concepts/truth\|truth]] dawned on me. Mrs. Guzzard!<br>Claude, Colby was brought up by a Mrs. Guzzard. |
| SIR CLAUDE | I know that. But why should that make him your son? |
| LADY ELIZABETH | It’s the name I’ve been hunting for all these years —<br>That, and the other name, Teddington:<br><br><br>Mrs. Guzzard of Teddington. That was all I knew.<br>Then Tony was killed, as you know, in Africa,<br>And I had lost the name. Mrs. Guzzard. |
| SIR CLAUDE | I’m beginning now to piece it together.<br><br><br>You’ve been asking Colby about his family … |
| LADY ELIZABETH | And when he mentioned Teddington, there was a faint echo —<br><br><br>And then Mrs. Guzzard! It must be true. |
| SIR CLAUDE | It is certainly a remarkable coincidence —<br><br><br>If it is a coincidence. But I’m afraid, Elizabeth,<br>What has happened is that, brooding on the past,<br>You began to think of Colby as what your son would be,<br>And then you began to see him as your son,<br>And then — any name you heard would have seemed the right one. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | Oh Claude, how can you be so sceptical!<br><br><br>We must see this Mrs. Guzzard, and get her to confess it. |
| SIR CLAUDE | I’m sorry, Elizabeth. If Mrs. Guzzard comes<br><br><br>To make her confession, it will be very different<br>From what you expect. I’m afraid, Colby,<br>It seems to me that we must let her know the [[concepts/truth\|truth]]. |
| COLBY | It seems to me … there is nothing for me —<br><br><br>Absolutely nothing — for me to say about it.<br>I must leave that to you. |
| SIR CLAUDE | I should have told you one day.<br><br><br>I’ve always loathed keeping such a thing from you.<br>I see now I might as [[motifs/well\|well]] have told you before,<br>But I’d hoped — and now it seems a silly thought …<br>What happens is so like what one had planned for,<br>And yet such a travesty of all one’s plans —<br>I’d hoped that you would become fond of Colby,<br>And that he might come to take the place of your own [[archetypes/child\|child]],<br>If you got to know him first — and that you’d want to adopt him. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | But of course I want to adopt him, Claude!<br><br><br>That is, if one’s allowed to adopt one’s own [[archetypes/child\|child]]. |
| SIR CLAUDE | That’s not what I meant. Elizabeth,<br><br><br>Colby is my son. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | Quite impossible, Claude!<br><br><br>You have a daughter. Now you want a son. |
| SIR CLAUDE | I’d never want to take your son away from you.<br>Perhaps you have a son. But it isn’t Colby.<br>I ought to have told you, years ago.<br>I told you about Lucasta, and you told me<br>About your own … misfortune. And I almost told you<br>About Colby. I didn’t. For such a foolish reason.<br>Absurd it sounds now. One [[archetypes/child\|child]] each —<br>That seemed fair enough — though yours had been lost,<br>And mine I couldn’t lose. But if I had another<br>I thought you might think — ‘and how many more?’<br>You might have suspected any number of children!<br>That seems [[concepts/grotesque\|grotesque]] now. But it influenced me.<br>And I found a better reason for keeping silent.<br>I came to see how you longed for a son of your own,<br>And I thought, I’ll wait for children of our own,<br><br><br>And tell her then. And they never came.<br>And now I regret the decision bitterly.<br>I ought to have told you that I had a son. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | But why do you think that Colby is your son? |
| SIR CLAUDE | Colby is the son of Mrs. Guzzard’s sister,<br><br><br>Who died when he was born. Mrs. Guzzard brought him up,<br>And I provided for his [[concepts/education\|education]].<br>I have watched him grow. And Mrs. Guzzard<br>Knows he is my son. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | But where were you, Claude,<br><br><br>When Colby was born? |
| SIR CLAUDE | Where was I? In Canada.<br><br><br>My father had sent me on a business tour<br>To learn about his overseas investments. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | Then how do you know that the sister had a [[archetypes/child\|child]]?<br><br><br>Perhaps Mrs. Guzzard invented the story…. |
| SIR CLAUDE | Why should she invent it? The [[archetypes/child\|child]] was expected. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | In order to get money from you, perhaps.<br><br><br>No, I shouldn’t say that. But she had a [[archetypes/child\|child]]<br>Left on her hands. The father had died<br>And she’d never been told the name of the mother;<br>And the mother had forgotten the name of Mrs. Guzzard,<br>And I was the mother and the [[archetypes/child\|child]] was Colby;<br>And Mrs. Guzzard thought you would be happy<br>To think you had a son, and would do [[motifs/well\|well]] by him —<br>Because you did care for the girl, didn’t you? |
| SIR CLAUDE | Yes, I did care. Very much. I had never<br><br><br>Been in [[motifs/love\|love]] before. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | Very [[motifs/well\|well]] then.<br><br><br>That is the way it must have happened.<br>Oh, Claude, you know I’m rather weak in the head<br>Though I try to be clever. Do try to help me. |
| SIR CLAUDE | It could have happened. But I’m sure it didn’t. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | Oh, Colby, doesn’t your instinct tell you? |
| SIR CLAUDE | Yes, tell us everything that’s in your mind.<br><br><br>I know this situation must be more of an agony<br>To you, than it can be even to … us. |
| COLBY | I only wish it was more acute agony:<br><br><br>I don’t know whether I’ve been [[concepts/suffering\|suffering]] or not<br>During this conversation. I only feel … numb.<br>If there’s agony, it’s part of a total agony<br>Which I can’t begin to feel yet. I’m simply indifferent.<br>And all the [[motifs/time\|time]] that you’ve been talking<br>I’ve only been thinking: ‘What does it matter<br>Whose son I am?’ You don’t understand<br>That when one has lived without parents, as a [[archetypes/child\|child]],<br>There’s a gap that never can be filled. Never.<br>I like you both, I could even come to [[motifs/love\|love]] you —<br>But as friends … older friends. Neither, as a parent.<br>I am sorry. But that’s why I say it doesn’t matter<br>To me, which of you should be my parent. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | But a mother, Colby, isn’t that different?<br><br><br>There should always be a bond between mother and son,<br>No matter how long they have lost each other. |
| COLBY | No, Lady Elizabeth. The position is the same<br><br><br>Or crueller. Suppose I am your son.<br>Then it’s merely a fact. Better not know<br>Than to know the fact and know it means nothing.<br>At the [[motifs/time\|time]] I was born, you might have been my mother,<br>But you chose not to be. I don’t blame you for that:<br>God forbid! but we must take the consequences.<br>At the [[motifs/time\|time]] when I was born, your being my mother —<br>If you are my mother — was a living fact.<br>Now, it is a dead fact, and out of dead facts<br>Nothing living can [[motifs/spring\|spring]]. Now, it is too late.<br>I never wanted a parent till now —<br>I never thought about it. Now, you have made me think,<br>And I wish that I could have had a father and a mother. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | Stop, Colby! Something has come to me.<br><br><br>Claude! I don’t want to take away from you<br>The son you thought was yours. And I know from what you said,<br>That you would rather he was ours than only yours.<br>Why should we make any further enquiries?<br>Let us regard him as being our son:<br><br><br>It won’t be the same as what we had wanted —<br>But in some ways better! And prevent us both<br>From making unreasonable claims upon you, Colby.<br>It’s a good idea! Why should we not be happy,<br>All of us? Already, Claude,<br>I feel as if this brought us closer together. |
| SIR CLAUDE | I should be contented with such an understanding;<br><br><br>And indeed, it’s not so far from what I had intended.<br>Could you accept us both in that way, Colby? |
| COLBY | I can only say what I feel at the moment:<br><br><br>And yet I believe I shall always feel the same. |
| SIR CLAUDE | [[motifs/well\|Well]]? |
| COLBY | It would be easier, I think,<br><br><br>To accept you both in the place of parents<br>If neither of you could be. If it was pure fiction —<br>One can live on a fiction — but not on such a mixture<br>Of fiction and fact. Already, it’s been hard<br>For me, who have never known the feelings of a son,<br>To be disputed between two parents.<br>But, if we followed your suggestion,<br>I know, I know I should always be haunted<br>By the miserable [[motifs/ghosts\|ghosts]] of the other parents!<br>It’s strange enough to have two parents —<br>But I should have four! What about those others?<br>I should have to live with those [[motifs/ghosts\|ghosts]], one indignant<br>At being cheated of his — or her — parenthood,<br>The other indignant at the imputation<br>Of false parenthood. Both mocked at. |
| SIR CLAUDE | Then what do you want, Colby? What do you want?<br><br><br>Think of the future. When you marry<br>You will want parents, for the sake of your children. |
| COLBY | I don’t feel, tonight, that I ever want to marry.<br>You may be right. I can’t take account of that.<br>But now I want to know whose son I am. |
| SIR CLAUDE | Then the first thing is: we must see Mrs. Guzzard. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | Oh Claude! I am terribly sorry for you.<br><br><br>I believe that if I had known of your … delusion<br>I would never have undeceived you. |
| SIR CLAUDE | And as for me,<br><br><br>If I could have known what was going to happen,<br>I would gladly have surrendered Colby to you.<br>But we must see Mrs. Guzzard. I’ll arrange to get her here. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | And I think you ought to get Eggerson as [[motifs/well\|well]].<br><br><br>SIR CLAUDE [rising]. Oh, of course, Eggerson! He knows all about it.<br><br><br>Let us say no more tonight. Now, Colby,<br>Can you find some consolation at the piano? |
| COLBY | I don’t think, tonight, the piano would help me:<br><br><br>At the moment, I never want to touch it again.<br>But there’s another reason. I must remind you<br>About your speech for the Potters’ Company<br>Tomorrow [[motifs/night\|night]]. I must get to work on it. |
| SIR CLAUDE | Tomorrow [[motifs/night\|night]]. Must I go to that dinner<br><br><br>Tomorrow [[motifs/night\|night]]? |
| COLBY | I was looking at your notes —<br><br><br>Before you brought me into the conversation —<br>And I found one note I couldn’t understand.<br>‘Reminiscent mood.’ I can’t develop that<br>Unless you can tell me — reminiscent of what? |
| SIR CLAUDE | Reminiscent of what? Reminiscent of what?<br><br><br>‘Tonight I feel in a reminiscent mood’ —<br>Oh yes. To say something of my early ambitions<br>To be a potter. Not that the Members<br>Of the Potters’ Company know anything at all<br>About ceramics … or any other art.<br>No, I don’t think I shall be in a reminiscent mood.<br>[[motifs/cross\|Cross]] that out. It would only remind me<br>Of things that would surprise the Potters’ Company<br>If I told them what I was really remembering.<br>Come, Elizabeth. |
| LADY ELIZABETH | My poor Claude! |
| *(didascalia)* | [Exeunt SIR CLAUDE and LADY ELIZABETH] |
| LADY ELIZABETH | CURTAIN |

| Chi parla | Battuta |  
|---|---|  
| *(didascalia)* | L’appartamentino nella corte, qualche settimana dopo. COLBY è seduto al pianoforte; LUCASTA in poltrona. Al levarsi del sipario si odono le ultime battute di un pezzo di musica. |  
| LUCASTA | Credo che tu suoni terribilmente [[motifs/well\|bene]], Colby —<br><br><br>non che il mio parere conti qualcosa:<br><br><br>lo sai. Ma vorrei imparare qualcosa di musica.<br>Vorrei che m’insegnassi ad apprezzarla. |  
| COLBY | Non credo che ti servirà molto insegnamento;<br><br><br>non a questo stadio, almeno. Tutto ciò che ti serve all’inizio<br>è ascoltare più musica. E scoprire che cosa ti piace.<br>Quando saprai che cosa ti piace, e comincerai a conoscerlo [[motifs/well\|bene]],<br>allora vorrai imparare la sua struttura<br>e le varie forme, e i diversi modi di eseguirla. |  
| LUCASTA | Ma se mi piacessero solo le cose sbagliate? |  
| COLBY | No, sono sicuro che preferirai quelle giuste, quando le sentirai.<br><br><br>Ti ho fatto una prova. Parecchi dei pezzi<br>che ti ho appena suonato erano di seconda scelta,<br>e non ti sono piaciuti. Ti sono piaciuti quelli giusti. |  
| LUCASTA | Colby, non sapevo che fossi così astuto!<br><br><br>Dunque le cose che mi sono piaciute erano quelle giuste?<br>Eppure sono terribilmente ignorante. Ci credi<br>che non sono mai stata a un concerto in vita mia?<br>Vado agli spettacoli solo quando qualcuno mi invita,<br>e nessuno mi ha mai invitata a un concerto.<br>All’Opera ci sono stata, certo, parecchie volte,<br>ma temo di non aver mai davvero ascoltato la musica:<br>mi piaceva solo andarci — per vedere gli altri,<br>e per farmi vedere! E perché ti senti tagliata fuori<br>se non vai mai all’Opera, in stagione.<br>Anche se mi sono sempre sentita tagliata fuori. E ti rendi conto<br>che nessuno ha mai suonato per me prima d’ora? |  
| COLBY | E questa è la prima [[motifs/time\|volta]] che io suono per qualcuno… |  
| LUCASTA | Non fare l’impostore. Lo sai che mi hai detto<br><br><br>che il pianoforte è stato consegnato solo questa settimana<br>e che l’hai fatto accordare ieri. Comunque, sono lusingata<br>di essere la tua prima visitatrice in questo appartamento<br>e la prima a sentirti suonare questo pianoforte. |  
| COLBY | Non è questo che intendevo. Intendo che non ho suonato<br><br><br>per nessuno da quando sono giunto alla conclusione<br>che non sarei mai diventato un musicista. |  
| LUCASTA | Ti è pesato, allora, suonare per me? |  
| COLBY | A dire il vero, credo di aver suonato meglio.<br><br><br>Non riesco a decidermi a suonare per gli altri,<br>e quando sono [[Alone\|solo]] non riesco a dimenticare<br>che suono soltanto per me stesso.<br>Ma con te non era né [[concepts/solitude\|solitudine]] né… gli altri. |  
| LUCASTA | Sono contenta di non essere «gli altri». Suonerai ancora per me<br><br><br>e m’insegnerai la musica? |  
| COLBY | Sì, certo che lo farò.<br><br><br>Ma sono sicuro che quando imparerai qualcosa di musica —<br>e non ci vorrà molto — e sentirai dei bravi esecutori,<br>ti accorgerai in fretta di quanto io suoni male. |  
| LUCASTA | Davvero, Colby, quante difficoltà sollevi!<br><br><br>Ma che ne diresti di portarmi a un concerto? |  
| COLBY | Proprio l’altro giorno ti ho invitata… |  
| LUCASTA | Ad andare a vedere quella commedia musicale americana! |  
| COLBY | [[motifs/well\|Beh]], ti avevo sentita dire che volevi vederla. |  
| LUCASTA | Ma non con te! |  
| COLBY | L’hai messo bene in chiaro.<br><br><br>Ma perché non con me? |  
| LUCASTA | Perché a te non piacciono —<br><br><br>le commedie musicali americane. Credi che sia un complimento<br>invitare una donna a qualcosa che a lei piacerebbe<br>quando lei sa che a te non piacerebbe? Non è un complimento:<br><br><br>è soltanto… degnarsi. Ma se mi inviti<br>a qualcosa che piace a te — quello sì è un complimento.<br><br><br>Vuol dire che vuoi educarmi. |  
| COLBY | Ma non sapevo<br><br><br>che tu volessi essere educata. |  
| LUCASTA | Non lo sapevo nemmeno io.<br><br><br>Ma volevo che tu volessi educarmi;<br>e ora comincio a credere che lo voglia anch’io. |  
| COLBY | [[motifs/well\|Bene]], allora t’invito al prossimo concerto… |  
| LUCASTA | Al prossimo a cui vuoi andare tu. |  
| COLBY | E forse mi lascerai raccontarti prima<br><br><br>del programma — o delle cose che voglio sentire.<br>Ti suonerò i temi, così li riconoscerai.<br>Meglio ancora, ti farò sentire i dischi al grammofono. |  
| LUCASTA | Preferirei che mi suonassi tu qualche brano, spiegandomelo.<br><br><br>Cominceremo subito la mia [[concepts/education\|educazione]]. |  
| COLBY | Sospetto che sia tu a educare me. |  
| LUCASTA | Colby, sei davvero pieno di sorprese!<br><br><br>Non ho mai conosciuto un uomo ignorante come te<br>e che pure sapesse tante cose che non sospetteresti.<br>Forse è per questo che mi piaci. |  
| COLBY | Non è proprio quella la ragione. |  
| LUCASTA | Oh, dunque credi che tu mi piaccia?<br><br><br>Non sapevo che fossi così presuntuoso. |  
| COLBY | No, non è presunzione — la ragione a cui penso.<br><br><br>È qualcosa di molto semplice. |  
| LUCASTA | Allora vorrei che me lo dicessi.<br><br><br>Perché io non lo so. |  
| COLBY | La prima [[motifs/time\|volta]] che ci siamo incontrati<br><br><br>ti sforzavi molto di dare un’impressione falsa.<br>E poi ti sei accorta di non esserci riuscita. |  
| LUCASTA | Ah, dunque cercavo di dare un’impressione falsa?<br><br><br>Che genere d’impressione cercavo di dare? |  
| COLBY | Questo in fondo non importa. Ma, per qualche ragione,<br><br><br>pensavi che io mi sarei fatto comunque un’impressione falsa.<br>Preferivi che fosse una di tua creazione<br>piuttosto che aspettare di vedere che cosa succedeva.<br>[[concepts/hope\|Spero]] che non ti dispiaccia: so che suona impertinente. |  
| LUCASTA | [[motifs/well\|Beh]], c’è una cosa che non hai ancora imparato,<br><br><br>ed è accorgerti di quando fai un complimento.<br>Quello era un complimento. E molto abile. |  
| COLBY | Ammetto che all’inizio ero molto disorientato<br><br><br>da te… e da B. |  
| LUCASTA | Oh, da me… e da B. |  
| COLBY | Solo più tardi,<br><br><br>dopo averti vista parecchie volte,<br>ho deciso che quella era solo la tua forma di [[concepts/self\|autodifesa]]. |  
| LUCASTA | Che cosa ti ha fatto pensare che fosse [[concepts/self\|autodifesa]]? |  
| COLBY | Perché non sapevi aspettare di vedere che cosa succedeva.<br><br><br>Hai paura di ciò che accadrebbe se lasciassi le cose a se stesse.<br>Salti — perché hai paura di essere spinta.<br>Credo che tu sia coraggiosa — e credo che tu sia spaventata.<br>Forse sei stata ferita gravemente, in qualche [[motifs/time\|momento]].<br>O almeno può esserci stato qualcosa nella tua vita<br>che ti ha derubata di ogni senso di sicurezza. |  
| LUCASTA | E sono certa che tu ce l’hai — il senso di sicurezza. |  
| COLBY | No, non l’ho nemmeno io. |  
| LUCASTA | Ecco, non ti credo.<br><br><br>Che cosa pensavo fino ad ora? Oh, è strano, no,<br>che man mano che si conosce meglio una persona<br>la si trovi per certi versi molto simile a sé,<br>in modi inattesi. E poi si cominciano<br>a scoprire differenze dentro la somiglianza.<br>Tu potrai sentirti insicuro, per certi versi —<br><br><br>ma la tua insicurezza non ha nulla a che fare con la mia. |  
| COLBY | In che cosa è diversa? |  
| LUCASTA | È difficile da spiegare.<br><br><br>Forse è qualcosa che la tua musica rappresenta.<br>Una cosa la so. Quando mi raccontasti per la prima volta<br>che disastro fu nella tua vita<br>scoprire che non saresti mai stato un buon musicista —<br>certo, non so se avessi ragione.<br><br><br>Per quel che ne so, puoi esserti sbagliato,<br>e forse potresti essere un grandissimo musicista:<br>ma non è questo il punto. Tu ne eri convinto;<br>e sentivi che la tua vita era crollata<br>e che dovevi imparare a fare qualcosa di diverso.<br>E così hai fatto domanda per il posto di Eggerson,<br>e hai deciso di darti agli affari<br>e di diventare uno come Claude… o come B. Mi dispiaceva,<br>mi dispiaceva molto per te. Ammiravo il tuo [[concepts/courage\|coraggio]]<br>nel guardare in faccia i fatti — o i fatti come li vedevi tu.<br>Eppure, per tutto quel [[motifs/time\|tempo]], scoprivo di invidiarti<br><br><br>e non sapevo perché! E adesso credo di saperlo.<br>È terribile per un uomo dover rinunciare<br>a una carriera su cui ha messo il cuore, ne sono certa:<br>ma tu hai perduto soltanto il mondo esteriore:<br>ti resta il tuo mondo interiore — un mondo più reale.<br>Ecco perché sei diverso da tutti noi:<br>hai il tuo [[motifs/garden\|giardino]] segreto, in cui puoi ritirarti<br>chiudendoti il cancello alle spalle. |  
| COLBY | E chiudermi il cancello alle spalle?<br><br><br>Sei sicura di non avere anche tu il tuo [[motifs/garden\|giardino]] segreto<br>da qualche parte, se solo riuscissi a trovarlo? |  
| LUCASTA | Se riuscissi a trovarlo!<br><br><br>No, il mio unico [[motifs/garden\|giardino]] è… una piazza pubblica sudicia<br>in un quartiere squallido di [[settings/london\|Londra]] — come quella dove abitavo<br>per un [[motifs/time\|tempo]], con mia madre. Non ho [[motifs/garden\|giardino]].<br>Faccio fatica perfino a sentirmi una persona:<br>nient’altro che un pezzetto di materia viva<br>che galleggia sulla superficie del Regent’s Canal.<br>Che galleggia, ecco. |  
| COLBY | Sei eccome una persona.<br><br><br>Sono certo che c’è un [[motifs/garden\|giardino]] da qualche parte anche per te —<br>per chiunque lo desideri quanto lo desideri tu. |  
| LUCASTA | E il tuo [[motifs/garden\|giardino]] è un [[motifs/garden\|giardino]]<br><br><br>in cui odi una musica che nessun altro potrebbe udire,<br>e i [[motifs/flowers\|fiori]] hanno un profumo che nessun altro potrebbe sentire. |  
| COLBY | Forse hai ragione, fino a un certo punto.<br><br><br>Eppure, sai, non mi è del tutto reale —<br>benché mi sia reale quanto… questo mondo.<br>Ma è proprio questo il guaio. Sembrano non avere legame.<br>Giro la chiave, varco il cancello,<br>ed eccomi lì… [[Alone\|solo]], nel mio «[[motifs/garden\|giardino]]».<br>[[Alone\|Solo]], ecco il punto. Per questo non è reale.<br>Sai, io credo che il [[motifs/garden\|giardino]] di Eggerson<br>sia più reale del mio. |  
| LUCASTA | Il [[motifs/garden\|giardino]] di Eggerson?<br><br><br>Che cosa ti fa pensare a Eggerson — proprio a lui? |  
| COLBY | [[motifs/well\|Beh]], lui si ritira nel suo [[motifs/garden\|giardino]] — alla lettera,<br><br><br>e anche nello stesso senso in cui io mi ritiro nel mio.<br>Ma lì non si sente [[Alone\|solo]]. E quando ne esce<br>ha zucchine, o barbabietole, o piselli… per la signora Eggerson. |  
| LUCASTA | Ti stai burlando di me? |  
| COLBY | Sono serissimo.<br><br><br>Voglio dire: il mio [[motifs/garden\|giardino]] non mi è meno irreale<br>del mondo fuori di esso. Se hai due vite<br>che non hanno assolutamente nulla a che fare l’una con l’altra —<br>[[motifs/well\|beh]], sono irreali entrambe. Ma per Eggerson<br>il suo [[motifs/garden\|giardino]] è parte di un unico mondo. |  
| LUCASTA | Ma tu che cosa vuoi? |  
| COLBY | Non essere [[Alone\|solo]] là dentro.<br><br><br>Se fossi religioso, Dio passeggerebbe nel mio [[motifs/garden\|giardino]]<br>e questo renderebbe reale il mondo di fuori<br>e accettabile, credo. |  
| LUCASTA | Suoni terribilmente religioso.<br><br><br>Non c’è altro modo di rendertelo reale? |  
| COLBY | È semplicemente il fatto di essere [[Alone\|solo]] là dentro<br><br><br>che lo rende irreale. |  
| LUCASTA | Non può entrarci nessun altro? |  
| COLBY | Non si può fare spedendo inviti:<br><br><br>dovrebbero semplicemente venire. E io non li vedrei arrivare.<br>Non udirei aprirsi il cancello.<br>Sarebbero semplicemente… lì, all’improvviso,<br>inaspettatamente. Camminando lungo un viale<br>mi accorgerei che qualcuno cammina con me.<br>È l’unico modo che trovo per dirlo. |  
| LUCASTA | Quanta paura si ha… di essere feriti! |  
| COLBY | Non è la ferita che darebbe fastidio,<br><br><br>ma il senso di desolazione che viene dopo. |  
| LUCASTA | Capisco che cosa intendi. Allora i [[motifs/flowers\|fiori]] appassirebbero<br><br><br>e la musica si fermerebbe. E i muri sarebbero abbattuti.<br>E ti ritroveresti in una zona devastata —<br>un cratere di bomba… erbacce… una piazza pubblica sudicia.<br>Ma non riesco a immaginare che accada a te.<br>Mi sembri così sicuro. Non solo nella tua musica —<br>quella ne è solo l’espressione. Non mi sembri<br>aver bisogno di nessuno. |  
| COLBY | È del tutto falso. |  
| LUCASTA | Ma tu hai qualcos’altro, che io non ho:<br><br><br>qualcosa di cui la musica è un… simbolo.<br>Vorrei davvero capire la musica,<br>non per poterne parlare,<br>ma… in parte per goderla… e per ciò che rappresenta.<br>Sai, sono un po’ gelosa della tua musica!<br>Quando la vedo come un mezzo di contatto con un mondo<br>più reale di qualsiasi mondo in cui io abbia mai vissuto.<br><br><br>E vorrei capire te. |  
| COLBY | Credo che tu già lo faccia,<br><br><br>meglio di… altre persone. E io voglio capire te.<br>Ma si arriva mai a capire qualcuno? |  
| LUCASTA | Trovo che tu sia molto scoraggiante:<br><br><br>lo fai apposta? |  
| COLBY | Non è questo che intendevo.<br><br><br>Intendevo che non c’è una fine nel capire una persona.<br>Tutto ciò che si può fare è capirla meglio,<br>starle dietro; così che, quando l’altro cambia,<br>tu possa capire il cambiamento appena avviene,<br>anche se non avresti potuto prevederlo. |  
| LUCASTA | Credo di stare cambiando.<br><br><br>Sono cambiata parecchio nelle ultime due ore. |  
| COLBY | E credo di stare cambiando anch’io. Ma forse ciò che chiamiamo cambiamento… |  
| LUCASTA | È capire meglio ciò che si è davvero.<br><br><br>E la ragione per cui questo accade, forse… |  
| COLBY | È che si comincia a capire un’altra persona. |  
| LUCASTA | Oh Colby, ora che cominciamo a capirci,<br><br><br>vorrei che tu sapessi un po’ di più di me.<br>Te lo sarai chiesto. |  
| COLBY | Me lo sarò chiesto?<br><br><br>No, non me lo sono chiesto. È tutto un mondo strano<br>per me, sai, quello in cui mi ritrovo.<br>Ma se intendi chiedermi delle tue… origini:<br>no. Sono stato curioso di sapere che cosa sei,<br>non chi sei, nel senso comune.<br>È questo che intendi? Ti ho semplicemente accettata. |  
| LUCASTA | Oh, è meraviglioso essere accettati!<br><br><br>Nessuno mi ha mai «semplicemente accettata» prima.<br>Certo, i fatti non contano, in un certo senso.<br>Ma ora che siamo arrivati a questo punto — tanto [[motifs/well\|vale]] che tu li conosca. |  
| COLBY | Ti direi volentieri tutto di me;<br><br><br>ma tu sai già quasi tutto quel che c’è da dire,<br>o da ciò che ti ho detto io<br>o da ciò che ho detto a B.; o da Sir Claude. |  
| LUCASTA | Claude non mi ha detto nulla di te;<br><br><br>non mi dice granché. E quanto a B. —<br>preferisco di gran lunga sentirlo da te. |  
| COLBY | C’è solo una cosa che non posso dirti.<br><br><br>Almeno, non ancora. Non mi è permesso dirla.<br>E riguarda i miei genitori. |  
| LUCASTA | Oh, capisco.<br><br><br>[[motifs/well\|Beh]], non credo che abbia importanza.<br>Ma io posso dirti tutto dei miei genitori:<br><br><br>anzi, ho intenzione di farlo. |  
| COLBY | Ha importanza nemmeno quello? |  
| LUCASTA | In un certo senso sì. Poco fa<br><br><br>hai detto, molto acutamente, che quando ci siamo conosciuti<br>hai capito che cercavo di dare un’impressione falsa.<br>Ora voglio dirti perché cercavo di farlo.<br>E con gli altri mi è sempre riuscito:<br>ho preso l’abitudine di dare quell’impressione.<br>È lì che B. mi è stato di grande aiuto —<br>alimenta quell’impressione. Ci crede a metà.<br>Ma sa tutto di me, e sa<br>che quello che certi uomini hanno pensato di me non era vero. |  
| COLBY | Che cosa non era vero? |  
| LUCASTA | Che fossi l’amante di Claude —<br><br><br>o che lo fossi stata, e fossi stata rifilata a B. |  
| COLBY | Non ho mai pensato una cosa simile! |  
| LUCASTA | Non hai mai pensato una cosa simile!<br><br><br>Non sono molti gli uomini che non l’avrebbero pensato.<br>Di B. non so. È molto generoso.<br>Non credo che gli sarebbe importato. Ma è anche molto acuto;<br>e ha indovinato la [[concepts/truth\|verità]] fin dal primo istante. |
